# Courses

#### Introduction to Life Sciences: Fundamentals of Life

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Introduction to Life Sciences: Fundamentals of Life [BE 101]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Fundamentals of Biochemistry, Genetics, Molecular Biology, and Cell Biology; Structure and Regulation of Genes; Structure and Function of Proteins; How DNA, Proteins, and different units combine together to integrate into cells; How the cells integrate into multicellular systems and organisms; Concepts in population biology: principles of macro- and microevolution, population genetics, and population dynamics; Role of an engineer in Biology

BE 101

#### Molecular Genetics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Course Content

# Molecular Genetics [BE 301]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Structure of nucleic acids, Transcription, Translation, DNA replication, DNA repair and recombination; Gene expression in bacteria and eukaryotes, regulatory sequences, activators, repressors, regulation of transcription factors, elongation and termination of transcription; Post-transcriptional gene control, Processing of pre-mRNA and regulation, transport of mRNA and degradation.

BE 301

#### Bionanotechnology – Principles and Applications

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Bionanotechnology – Principles and Applications [BE 303]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction to Nanotechnology and special focus on Bionanotechnology.

Nanotechnology with molecules of life: Proteins-Proteins Structures, Designs, Nanomachines, Origami designs and Applications; Carbohydrates-Glyconanotechnology, Applications in Glycobiology, vaccination, delivery and cell biology; Lipids-Structure, function, designs, membranes and applications in drug delivery formulations and cell biology; Combinatorial Bionanotechnology-Hybrid formulations, structures, functions and applications.

DNA Nanotechnology: Structure of DNA- Why DNA is genetic material of life?; Naturally occuring DNA nanomachines; Discovery of DNA nanotechnology - Initial designs and validations; Multi-arm DNA based 1/2/3D nanodevices; DNA Origami; Non-canonical Motifs and applications-Aptamers, i-motif, G-quadruplexes.

Applications of DNA Nanotechnology: Cell Engineering; Theranostics Diagnostics (Biosensing) and therapeutic applications; Biomedical Applications-Bioimaging including super-resolution imaging and single-particle tracking; DNA based computing and robotics.

BE 303

#### Introduction to Biomedical Engineering

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Introduction to Biomedical Engineering [BE 304]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This course will provide an introductory overview of the multi-disciplinary field of biomedical engineering. We will discuss applications of elementary engineering principles to the analyses of physiological systems for addressing challenges in health care. The course topics will include the following:

 1. Biomedical engineers and modern health care systems: Addressing unmet clinical needs, interfacing with clinicians, research, and development
 2. Overview of anatomy and physiology: Cellular organization, tissues, major organ systems, communication systems, respiration, digestion, circulation, removal of molecules, homeostasis
 3. Biomechanics: Basic mechanics (elasticity, viscosity, deformation and viscoelasticity), mechanics of tissues (e.g., cartilage, ligaments, tendon, muscle), physics and mechanics of locomotion (e.g., walking, running, swimming), design in biomechanics and orthopedics
 4. Biomaterials: Materials in medicine, lessons from nature on biomaterial design and selection, tissue-biomaterial interactions, safety testing
 5. Tissue engineering: Challenges in organ transplantation, cell culture, regulation of healing processes, replacement of diseased tissues, tissue engineering in control of drug delivery, artificial organs
 6. Biosignals: Acquiring and analyzing biosignals, electrocardiogram (ECG), electroencephalography (EEG), electromyogram (EMG), thermography, blood flow analysis
 7. Brief overview of imaging: Optical microscopy, X-Rays, computed tomography, magnetic resonance, ultrasound, and nuclear medicine
 8. Medical devices: Classes of medical devices, considerations for design and development, introduction to regulatory processes in Indian, U.S., and European markets, case studies from India and abroad
 9. Bioethics: Animal and human experimentation, informed consent, euthanasia, ethical issues in feasibility studies, ethical clearance

BE 304

#### Origins of Species

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Origins of Species [BE 305]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Hierarchical organization of life, the concept of species; Morphological, phenotypic and molecular bases of species; Concept of species in asexually and sexually reproducing organisms; Genomes and species; Population sizes, genetic diversity within species; genetic intermixing of closely related species, positive and negative effects of hybrids between different species; Habitats, ecological niche separation, adaption and selection; Reproductive barriers and roles of sex chromosomes; Reproductive compatibilities and incompatibilities; Classical genetic techniques to study speciation; classical and modern molecular mechanisms of speciation.

BE 305

#### Methods in Biology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 0 | 0 | 3 | 2

 Course Content

# Methods in Biology [BE 401]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
0 | 0 | 3 | 2

 Basic laboratory techniques that are an integral part of a Biological Engineering program will be explored in detail via laboratory experiments. This is a laboratory course in which we will conduct experiments in Molecular biology including PCR, restriction enzyme digestion, gel electrophoresis, cloning, ligation, transformation, Protein expression & purification in prokaryotic systems (e.g. affinity chromatography, size exclusion chromatography) and characterization of proteins by SDS- PAGE, Western blotting.

BE 401

#### Stem Cells: Science and Applications

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Stem Cells: Science and Applications [BE 403]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 1. Introduction to Stem Cells: Basic overview of stem cells--what they are, the history of stem cell research, and the potential for stem cell therapies. Stem cells are found throughout the tree of life. 2. How do Stem Cells work: How to induce stem cells to differentiate into particular cell types? New stem cell-assisted technologies such as mitochondrial replacement therapy and generating chimeras for organ transplantation. Background information on the science and ethics of cloning, gene editing methods with special focus on gene editing in stem cells. Combining genome editing and stem cell engineering as link that explores the use of stem cells to understand disease and the use of stem cells as therapeutics. 3. Using Stem Cells to study Diseases: How cultured cells can be used to understand the cause of diseases and to look for drugs that will potentially cure them. We will also explore how stem cells have the potential to help with the study of diseases like hematological disorders, diabetes and Alzheimer's. 4. Using Stem Cells to Treat Diseases: Explore the potential for using stem cells in our own bodies (adult stem cells) to treat hematological disorders, age-related macular degeneration, neuronal-degeneration and like diseases. Some tools for evaluating potential stem cell treatments will be covered in details. 5. The Bioethics of Stem Cell Research and Therapy: Discuss some of the controversies surrounding human embryonic stem cells and the legal, policy, and ethical challenges associated with the use of stem cells in basic and clinical research. Discussion about the future of reproductive technology and stem cell research.

BE 403

#### Genetic Engineering – Principles and Applications

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Genetic Engineering – Principles and Applications [BE 404]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Module a. Introduction to genetic engineering + Fundamentals of molecular and cell biology
 Brief Introduction to the course and area of genetic engineering, Transcription, Translation, DNA replication and Recombination.
Module b. Introduction to Recombinant DNA technology
 Polymerase Chain Reaction, Fundamental techniques of gene manipulation, Basic principles of gene cloning, Vectors for gene cloning, Purification of DNA from living cells, Isolation of desired Gene, Manipulation of purified DNA.
Module c. Introduction of DNA into living cells
 Transgenics, chimeras, somatic and germ-line gene delivery, site directed mutagenesis, therapeutic reverse mutations, production of recombinant proteins in E.coli and other organisms.
Module d: Principles of Gene editing
 CRISPR-Cas9 technology, genetic manipulation of microbes, plants and animals.
Module e: Applications of gene cloning and DNA analysis in Biotechnology
 Gene cloning and DNA analysis in Medicine, Gene cloning and DNA analysis in Agriculture, Gene Cloning and DNA analysis in Forensic science and Archaeology, Ethical issues and concerns related to Genetically modified organisms.

BE 404

#### Introduction to Plant Biotechnology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Introduction to Plant Biotechnology [BE 405]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Module a. Introduction to Origin of plant biotechnology
 History and development of plant biotechnology
Module b. Introduction to laboratory organization and techniques in tissue culture
 Laboratory organization methods and criteria; introduction to plant tissue culture; plasticity and totipotency; nutrition medium, sterilization techniques; cytodifferentiation; organogenic differentiation; types of cultures: seed culture, embryo culture, callus culture, organ culture, cell culture, protoplast culture; Micropropogation, generation of virus free plants; plant regeneration (somatic embryogenesis); cell suspension and secondary metabolites; invitro production of haploids (anther culture, microspore culture
Module c. Special techniques in plant biotechnology
 Protoplast isolation; somatic hybridization; embryo rescue; somaclonal variation; germplasm storage and cryopreservation
Module d: Transgenic technology for crop improvement
 Recombinant DNA technology; strategies for cloning genes from plants; gene transfer in plants; transgenics in crop improvement; current state of transgenic crops and GM crop regulation
Module e. Beyond genetically modified crops
 Genome editing; genetic mapping; insertional mutagenesis and tilling; functional genomics.

BE 405

#### Systems Biology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Systems Biology [BE 406]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction to mathematical modeling of biological systems-
 Networks in biological systems- Reconstruction of networks from experimental data, Network motifs and their role in autoregulation, feed forward loops. Specific biological networks and their modeling- Transcription networks- Activators, Repressors, Input functions and Dynamics; Metabolic networks- Rate laws, Flux Analysis, Signal transduction networks- Static models and dynamic models.
 Robustness in biological circuits- Principle of robustness with examples of robustness in biological systems, detailed analysis of bacterial chemotaxis as robust biochemical system, Stochasticity in biological processes- introduction to stochastic modeling.
 Design of biological systems- Metabolic Engineering, Case Studies- synthetic biology, Emerging topics in systems biology- precision medicine, biomarker discovery using systems biology

BE 406

#### Introduction to Cell Biology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Introduction to Cell Biology [BE 407]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Module a. Origin of Cell Biology
 History and development of Cell Biology; Invention of microscopes; Discovery of cells; Cell theory; Membrane and bulk phase theories; Steady-state membrane pump concept
 Module b. Cell structure and organization
 Cell as a unit of life, prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells, difference between plant and animal cells, bio-membranes, Structure and functions of various cell organelles i.e., nucleus, ribosome, ER, golgi, lysosomes, peroxisome, exosomes, vacuoles, chloroplasts, cytoskeleton; Organization of tissues: Cell-cell and cell matrix interactions, cell adhesion molecules, composition of extracellular matrix, cellular junctions and role; Cellular movement: types of movement, cilia and flagella, muscle contraction, role of cytoskeletal proteins in movement
 Module c. How cells obtain energy from food
 Glycolysis, Citric acid cycle, Energy generation in mitochondria and chloroplasts, oxidative phosphorylation, electron transport, photosynthesis
 Module d: Cell cycle, Control of Cell Numbers and Cell Size
 G1, G2, S and M phase of the cell cycle, Cell cycle analysis and its applications, Programed cell death apoptosis vs necrosis, Mitogens stimulating cell division, growth factors
 Module e: Transport systems in cells
 Osmosis, active and passive transport, protein transporters, ion channels, protein sorting, vesicular transport, secretory pathways, endocytic pathways, exocytosis, micropinocytosis, role of cytoskeletal proteins in transport, molecular motors
 Module f: Cell signaling
 Principles of cell signaling, Concept or receptor-ligand, Signalling molecules, Receptor types: ion-channel-coupled receptor, G-protein Receptor, Enzyme coupled receptors
 Module g: Techniques used in cell biology and relevance of cell biology
 Histology, staining, microscopy, TEM and SEM, live cell imaging, Fluorescent dyes and genetically encoded fluorescent tags, cell culture, FACS; Understanding Cancer, Stem cells and application, Tissue engineering, Infectious diseases, manipulating cells for therapeutic purposes

BE 407

#### Applied Biological Engineering

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Applied Biological Engineering [BE 601]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Advanced themes that are an integral part of a modern Biological Engineering graduate program will be explored in detail. A representative list of topics is given below:
-  Nucleic acid in disease diagnosis: Detection of TB and Malaria by PCR, diagnosis of HIV by qPCR, monitoring flu evolution and hereditary genetic tests.
-  Antibiotic resistance: Prevalence and development of resistance in microbes. New approaches to identification and management of resistance.
-  Recombinant DNA technologies: Cloning vectors, mutagenesis methods, manipulation of gene expression in prokaryotes, introduction to eukaryotic gene expression systems.
-  DNA forensics: Sample collection, DNA extraction methods, DNA database searches, role of bioinformatics, low level and degraded DNA detection.
-  Waste water treatment and microbes: Aerobic and anaerobic oxidation, role of activated sludge, microbial ecology of a water treatment plant, genetically engineered microbes.
-  Biotechnology and clean energy: Renewable sources of energy, biofuel production by microbes, hydrogen producing biological systems and challenges.
-  Protein Engineering: Protein folding and recognition for protein design principles, Structure- Based Combinatorial Protein Engineering (SCOPE), proteomics.
-  Cancer Diagnostics: Diagnostics Imaging; X-rays, CAT-scan (computerized axial tomography), Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), ultrasound. Tumor Markers Microarrays in Cancer Diagnostics, Mass Spectrometry in Cancer Diagnostics.

BE 601

#### Biophysical Techniques

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 1 | 4

 Course Content

# Biophysical Techniques [BE 603]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 1 | 4

 Molecular structure determination: Part A: X-ray crystallography, the single most important technique used in determining the 3-D structure of macromolecules: Structural elucidation of proteins, Nucleic acids (DNA). Crystallographic theory, exercises on crystallization, data processing and model building (Docking, related software). Part B: Biophysical techniques are covered that supplement the 3-D characterization of biological macromolecules: NMR (Amino Acids, Mononucleotides, Proteins and Nucleic Acids) and mass spectroscopy (Fragmentation studies: A tool for structural elucidation of proteins).

BE 603

BE 607

#### Molecular Basis of Neurodegenerative Diseases

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Molecular Basis of Neurodegenerative Diseases [BE 608]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Overview of neurodegenerative diseases (NDs) including Alzheimer’s Disease (AD), Parkinson’s Disease (PD), Huntington’s Disease (HD), Progressive supranuclear palsy (PSP) and prion disease; Pathological events in human neurodegeneration; Genetic variation in inherited and sporadic disease; Role of pathogenic proteins and their aggregation, Inter and intracellular transmission of toxic species including Prion mechanism; Other prevalent disease mechanism hypotheses: Abnormal post- translational modifications, ubiquitin proteasome system (UPS) impairment, autophagy dysfunction, ,oxidative stress and inflammation, microbial infection; Development of therapeutic approaches including genome editing methods: latest trends and future perspective

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Nature of a cancer cell, similarities and differences from a normal cell; Molecular biology of cell growth, cycling and proliferation in oncogenic transformation (oncogenes and TSGs); Experimental and spontaneous cell transformation; Genetic and Epigenetic bases of cancer; Cancer initiating/stem cells; Genomic and epigenomic integrity vis-a-vis tumorigenesis; Stress and cancer cell survival (senescence, apoptosis and other forms of cell death in tumor environment); Cell immortality and tumorigenesis; Cellular and animal models of cancer.

 Credit
 L | T | P | C

 Biology by numbers: Size and geometry of cells and organelles, concentration of cellular building blocks, Energy currencies; Cellular processes at rest: Mechanical and Chemical Equilibrium of biomolecules, Entropy considerations, Random walks and the structure of Macromolecules, Electrostatics of intra and extracellular salty solutions, Biological membranes; Dynamics of cellular processes: A statistical view of biological dynamics, Water and diffusion, Rate equations, dynamics of molecular motors, Light and Electricity in biological systems; Organization of Biological Networks; Biological Patterns in space and time.

BE 612

#### Biochemistry

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Biochemistry [BE 613]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Biomolecules: Chemical bonding, structure and function of carbohydrates, proteins, lipids and nucleic acids, water and its properties.
 Synthesis, separation and purification: biochemistry of cell membrane, isomerism, subcellular fractions and their identification, protein separation and purification from different host system, disorders of carbohydrate and lipid metabolism, peptide synthesis, protein synthesis and post-translation modifications and it importance, protein engineering, protein lithography and its applications.
 Enzymes and their kinetics: enzymes, enzyme kinetics and enzyme regulation, enzyme engineering and its applications.
 Metabolism disorders: Inborn errors of metabolism, digestive diseases, disorders of liver and kidney, principles of thermodynamics, bioenergetics and oxidative phosphorylation. Blood clotting, electrolytes and acid-base balance, biological oxidation and muscle contraction.
 Nucleic acids: Structure and function of nucleotides, primary, secondary and tertiary structure of nucleic acids, DNA and RNA forms and conformations and their biological significance, denaturation and renaturation of DNA and RNA, in vivo and in vitro synthesis of DNA and RNA and their applications.

BE 613

#### Biostatistics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Course Content

# Biostatistics [BE 614]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 The course is aimed at providing an understanding of statistical techniques used to analyze biological and clinical data. The following topics will be covered:
 1. Making sense of and comparing biological datasets (Probability distributions, description of a population, measures of central tendency and dispersion, hypothesis testing, analysis of variance, nonparametric methods, correlation, and regression) 2. Rates and standardization (Rates, Methods for standardization of rates, cross- sectional vs longitudinal studies, person-time observations, Inference on person- time data) 3. Life tables (Computation of life tables, applications of life tables, years of potential life lost) 4. Survival Analysis (life table method, product-limit method, log-rank test) 5. Clinical Trials (randomization, sample size calculation for clinical trials, statistical power, inference on proportions) 6. Genetic Epidemiology (Twin studies, association studies, genome-wide association studies, Manhattan plots, rare variant studies) 7. Risk (risk prediction, additive value of biomarkers, net reclassification index, integrated discrimination improvement) 8. Scale construction and measurement (reliability, validity, responsiveness) 9. Measures of performance of diagnostic tests in clinical practice (Receiver operating characteristic curves, sensitivity, specificity, positive and negative predictive values, positive and negative likelihood ratios) 10. Common pitfalls and ethics in biostatistics reporting 11. Ethics in biological research (dealing with biological/human samples, research integrity, informed consent, safety, privacy and data monitoring, rigor, reproducibility)

BE 614

#### Bioinformatics and Computational Biology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Course Content

# Bioinformatics and Computational Biology [BE 615]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Biological Data- origin (experimental, such as genome sequencing, macromolecular structure determination; and theoretical, such as structure prediction, mathematical modeling), type, representation, analysis and storage.
 Biological sequence analysis- databases- querying and retrieval, alignment- pairwise and multiple, Hidden Markov models, Phylogenetic trees- Bayesian and Maximum likelihood methods, RNA sequence and structure. Genome sequencing, Introduction to proteomics, transcriptomics and metagenomics
 Going 3D- Protein structure, function and evolution, databases (query and retrieval), alignment, protein structure prediction (secondary and tertiary)- conventional and recent deep learning methods, coevolution, protein folding, Introduction to in-silico drug discovery.
 Introduction to systems biology- biological networks- protein interaction networks, regulatory networks.
 Basics of biological computing- Python, Biopython and R- specific only to biological data analysis.

BE 615

#### Human Physiology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Human Physiology [BE 616]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

environment, cellular homeostasis, Transmembrane transport, active and passive.

Structure of a neuron, membrane potential, excitation and inhibition of nerve cells and signal propagation/conduction across axons, signal transmission between neurons or neurons and target cells.

Skeletal muscle and neuromuscular junction organization, Skeletal Muscle contraction, Excitation-Contraction Coupling, Functioning of smooth and cardiac muscle cells.

The Heart as a Pump, cardiac input and output volumes, cardiac blood pressure and its regulation, SA node function and its regulation, signal conduction from SA node to cardiac muscles, PQRS waves and basics of a normal electrocardiogram.

Architecture of the pulmonary and systemic circulations, arteries, veins, portal systems, peripheral blood pressure and its regulation. Lung-alveolar structural organization, Pulmonary Ventilation and regulation of Gas Exchange, Oxygen and CO2 transport, haemoglobin and RBCs, Chloride shift.

Capillaries, lymphatic system, lymphatic flow and functions. Body fluid compartments, Oedema, formation of urine, regulation of ion balance and pH. Cellular and molecular bases of blood fluidity and clotting. Basics of innate, acquired immunity, cellular and humoral immunity.

Neuroendocrine system (growth hormone, adrenal glands, thyroid hormones and reproduction-associated hormones), Hypothalamus-pituitary axis.

Structural organization of the digestive tract, digestion (carbohydrates, proteins and fats), absorption and transport, utilisation for energy or storage, insulin, glucagon, liver-muscle-adipose crosstalk in energy metabolism and lipoprotein-cholesterol transport, regulation of body core temperature.

BE 616

#### Novel Drug Delivery Technologies: Fundamental Principles and Engineering

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Novel Drug Delivery Technologies: Fundamental Principles and Engineering [BE 617]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Overview of drug delivery: Current landscape of therapeutics and medicinal products. Challenges in the development of new therapeutics. Processes of conventional drug delivery, dosage forms of drugs, delivery routes, biological barriers (Skin, systemic, and local) and toxicity.
Novel Drug Delivery Technologies: Comprehensive overview of novel drug delivery technologies. Evolution of drug delivery technologies. Mechanisms of drug release (Drug release kinetics: Diffusion, degradation, bioerosion, swelling). Types of novel drug delivery technologies (Controlled drug delivery technologies, targeted drug delivery technologies (Passive and active, bio-responsive drug delivery technologies (pH, enzyme, and light), progress (Intra-articular drug delivery systems, local depots, and local drug therapies for bacterial infections), and promises.
Wrap-up: Translational and clinical aspects of drug delivery technologies. Present and future advancements of drug delivery technologies. Development process of novel drug delivery technologies from concept to market. Regulatory, intellectual property consideration, industry and start-up opportunities.

BE 617

# Molecular Biotechnology [BE 618]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

Case studies highlighting the applications of biotechnology in medicine, agriculture, forensic science, Archaeology and Evolutionary Biology; Genetic Engineering: Molecular cloning, generation of recombinant DNA, DNA transfer into living cells, Protein expression systems, purification of recombinant proteins, transgenics; Genetic manipulation: mutagenesis methods, targeted genome engineering, genome-scale screens.

#### Methods in Structural Biology

# Methods in Structural Biology [BE 627]

 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 1 | 4

 Course Content

# Nanoscale Science [BS 401]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

# Design of Reinforced Concrete Structures [CE 311]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C

 Introduction to Reinforced Concrete Structures and Design Philosophy: Working stress (WS) and limit state (LS) design philosophy; Factor of safety vs partial safety factor; and Characteristic strength vs characteristic load; Design of Typical Structural Components: Beams (WS and LS methods; Singly vs doubly reinforced; and Flexure and shear actions); Slabs (One-way and Two-way; Deflection check; and Yield line theory); Columns (Columns under axial load and bi-axial moment; P-M-M interaction; and Slender columns and secondary effects); Foundation (Isolated footing with axial and uniaxial / biaxial moment; and Combined footing); Staircase (Dog-legged; and Three flights); and Shear walls (per IS 13920); Introduction to Seismic Design: Seismic load as per IS 1893; Capacity Design per IS 13920; and Design of beam-column joints; Introduction to Design of Flat-Slab and Raft Foundation (Structural Design): Equivalent frame method; and Analysis using FE software; Introduction to Prestressed Concrete Design: Philosophy; Losses in prestressing; and Design of prestressed concrete beam; Testing of Cement: Standard consistency, Initial and final setting time and Soundness. CE 633

#### Air Pollution Control Engineering

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Air Pollution Control Engineering [CE 634]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Overview of air pollution: Types of air pollutants (classified into two categories): (1) Particulate matter (PM) and (2) gaseous pollutants (volatile organic compounds, sulfur oxides, nitrogen oxides, ozone, CO2); common sources; health and environmental impacts of air pollutants; the global and national context of air pollution: evolution of policies and regulations and the current status

Particulate Matter (PM): Particle size distribution and size-dependent transport properties such as settling, impaction, interception, and diffusion/Brownian motion; cyclones and impactors: operating principle, design, and sizing; electrostatic precipitator (ESP): operating principle, drift velocity, collection efficiency, and design and sizing of ESPs (including area, number of plates, and sections); fabric filters: operating principle, surface velocity, collection efficiency, and its temporal variation; baghouse filters: types of baghouse filters, design, and sizing, including size and number of bags and operating schedule; face mask and introduction to airborne transmission of coronavirus (an example of bio-particulate matter); scrubbers: operating principle and design; applications of the same principles for COVID and other airborne disease mitigation

Sulfur oxides and nitrogen oxides: Elementary oxidation-reduction chemistry; fuel desulfurizations; limestone scrubbing; thermal and fuel NOx; combustion modification as a control strategy for NOx

Volatile organic compounds (VOCs): Review of fundamentals of vapor pressure, multi-phase equilibrium, and diffusivity; oxidation chemistry and kinetics of VOC incineration; design and sizing of thermal oxidizers; VOC prevention (substitution, process modification, and leakage control or fugitive emissions); VOC capture via adsorption: physical and chemical adsorption and isotherms; fixed-bed adsorption system: design and sizing and breakthrough curves (operating schedule); VOC capture via absorption: design and sizing of absorption tower

CO2 and ozone (O3): Global warming effects of CO2; CO2 capture technologies; Overview of CO2 sequestration; atmospheric chemistry of ozone and interplay with other pollutants; photochemical smog

Air pollution and meteorology: Wind circulations; lapse rates and inversions; atmospheric dispersion modeling; Gaussian plume model (downwind concentration and ground-level concentration from a point source emissions); plume rise and stack height determination

Other topics to be covered: Mercury control; mobile sources (motor vehicles): IC engine and diesel engine operation, air-fuel ration, formation of pollutants, catalytic converter; indoor air quality: differences from outdoor air, sources, material balance

# AI for Civil Engineering [CE 639]

CE 639

#### Hydrodynamics of Sediment Transport

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Hydrodynamics of Sediment Transport [CE 640]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Sediment properties: Physical properties of sediment, sediment sizes, sediment mixture, suspended sediment mixture, terminal fall velocity of sediment in fluid; Turbulent flow characteristics: Introduction to turbulence, decomposition and averaging procedure, Reynolds-averaged Navier–Stokes (RANS) equations, Reynolds shear stress in a zero-pressure gradient flow, the law of the wall, the log-wake law and the velocity defect law, secondary currents and dip phenomenon; Sediment threshold: Definition of sediment threshold, threshold velocity concept, Yang’s threshold velocity model, lift force concept, threshold bed shear stress concept, Shields diagram, probabilistic concept of sediment entrainment, turbulence-induced concept of sediment entrainment, stable channel design, straight trapezoidal channels, stable-ideal section of a threshold channel; Bedload transport: Definition of bedload transport, bed shear stress concept of bedload transport, du Boys’ approach, du Boys-type equations, discharge concept of bedload transport, velocity concept of bedload transport, bedform concept of bedload transport, probabilistic concept of bedload transport, Einstein’s approach, Engelund and Fredsøe’s approach, deterministic concept of bedload transport, Bagnold’s approach, Yalin’s approach, saltation, saltation characteristics, particle trajectory and characteristic parameters, streambed armoring and water-worked bed; Suspended-load transport: Diffusion concept of suspended-load transport, generalized advection–diffusion equation for suspended sediment motion, governing equation for sediment concentration distribution, various approaches to determine sediment concentration distribution, reference level and reference concentration, computation of suspended load by diffusion approach, energy concept of suspended-load transport, wash load, turbidity current; Total-load transport: Indirect approach, direct approach, total-load transport of nonuniform sediment; River processes: Bed forms, bars, prediction of bedforms, mathematical developments on sand waves, bed features in gravel-bed streams, resistance to flow due to bedforms, meandering rivers, braided rivers; Scour: Scour within channel contractions, scour downstream of structures, scour below horizontal pipelines, scour at bridge piers, scour at bridge abutments, scour countermeasures

CG 506

#### Evolutionary Neuropsychology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Course Content

# Evolutionary Neuropsychology [CG 507]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Paleoneuropsychology is the study the evolution of the structures and functions of the human brain. It is an emerging multidisciplinary science that spans the fields of anthropology, archaeology, cognitive neuroscience, and psychology. This course is designed as introduction to the specific structures and functions of the human brain with particular attention to empirical brain research advances since 2000. It is also designed as an introduction to the evolution of the human brain (size, shape, neurons, chemical neurotransmitters, etc.) and the evolution of human cognitive processes such as memory, language, dreaming, and creativity.

CG 507

#### Behavioral Economics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Behavioral Economics [CG 513]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction: Historical development of the field, interdisciplinary perspectives linking psychology, economics and neuroscience, Overview of the field with real life examples. Modes of thought, Heuristics and biases: Modes of thought (intuitive and deliberative), heuristics (anchoring and availability, representativeness), Biases (Framing effects, mental accounting), taming intuitive heuristics, self-regulation in the brain, The positive side of heuristic decision making. Choice under certainty - concept of preference in rational choice theory, revealed preference, decision making under certainty. Choice under uncertainty – The concept of value and utility under uncertainty, psychological weighting of probabilities, deciding about prospects, gains and losses, Neural representations of subjective value and choices in the brain. Thinking over time - Discounted utility, hyperbolic discounting, affective forecasting biases, retrospective experiential biases for value computation. Applying behavioral economics - Marketing examples from prospect theory, choice architecture, Real examples of nudges for better health, wealth and happiness.

CG 513

#### Introduction to Cognitive Linguistics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Introduction to Cognitive Linguistics [CG 517]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Basics of Cognitive Linguistics: This module introduces theoretical assumptions and commitments of Cognitive Linguistics, a functional approach to language use contrasted by formal approaches. In that sense, a fundamental question is asked: “What does it mean to know a language?” Also, two main aspects of Cognitive Linguistics—Language and Cognition—are interpreted; Linguistic Relativity: Beginning with the Sapir-Whorf hypothesis, this module taps into uniformity and diversity across languages in regard to the associated processing and behavior. The main discussion is on cross-linguistic variations in conceptualizations of time, space, and color; Categorization: Projection of how mental lexicon and concepts operate in the human mind. To understand the hierarchy of concepts, the association of lexical items as well as lexicon-internal polysemy are explored. Theories including the prototype theory are discussed and compared. It leads to the semantic basis of grammatical category; Mental Spaces: Cases of metaphor, metonymy, meronomy, and blends are analyzed with cross-linguistic points of view. The discussion components include the Conceptual Metaphor Theory and the Mental Space Theory; Cognitive Approaches to Grammar: This module discusses the structural consideration of human language processing. It covers idioms, Construction grammar, and Cognitive grammar.

CG 517

#### Motor Learning and Memory

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Motor Learning and Memory [CG 601]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Motor systems in the brain – motor and premotor cortices, parietal and frontal cortices, basal ganglia and cerebellum; Approaches to studying motor learning – defining and measuring motor learning, designing experiments on learning, alternative methods for measuring learning, issues about “amount” of learning, understanding differences between learning and changes in performance; Learning of motor sequences – implicit versus explicit learning, chunking, role of sleep, neural basis, disruption in different brain disorders; Adaptation learning – adaptation to novel dynamical and visual perturbations, sensory signals responsible for adaptation, generalization and transfer of learning, elementary computational models, neural basis; Learning under unstable and unpredictable conditions – motor noise, impedance control, feedforward and feedback components of impedance control; Motor skill learning – use dependent learning, “model-free” reinforcement learning, neural basis; Vocal learning in songbirds – role of variability and use of exploration, critical period, neural circuits, application to human speech learning; Application to neurorehabilitation and robotics – motor learning principles in rehab, robot-assisted rehab, error augmentation strategies, model of motor recovery after stroke, concurrent force and impedance adaptation in robots, robotic implementation.

CG 601

#### Classics in Brain Science

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Course Content

# Classics in Brain Science [CG 602]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Half semester course examining, at an advanced level, classic research that has shaped current understanding of brain function. Following topics will be covered: 1. Generation and transmission of neuronal signals ( 2. Intercellular communication 3. Vision 4. Proprioception (Processing of somatosensory information and organization of somatosensory cortex. Classic experiments by Vallbo, Matthews and Willis) 5. Spinal Reflexes (Classic experiments on reflex circuit organization by Sherrington, which won him the Nobel prize; work by Matthews, Eccles and Lundberg on reflex physiology). 6. Control of voluntary movement (organization of motor cortex, descending projections from motor cortical areas, movement planning and control all examined via studies of Evarts, Georgopoulos, Strick and Kalaska) 7. Learning and Memory (Cortical basis of learning and memory, two learning/memory systems, retention and recall of memories as explained by the definitive work of Squire, Poldrack and Nobel prize winner Kandel) Modern techniques used in research in the above areas will be introduced alongside the discussion on each topic.

CG 602

#### Perception and Attention

CG 603

#### Phenomenology, Embodiment, and Consciousness

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Phenomenology, Embodiment, and Consciousness [CG 604]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This course introduces Phenomenology, the various forms that it takes (Transcendental, Genitive, Existential, Feminist, Post-, Neuro), its emphasis on description (particularly first- person experience), and the role of the body in consciousness. Phenomenology as a method is oriented around what is called the ‘époché’ or bracketing (phenomenological reduction), wherein the subjective concerns of an individual are placed ‘in brackets’ in order to move toward an accurate description of the things themselves. We will discuss whether or not such a bracketing is possible, the role of Phenomenology in description and pay particularly close attention to concepts such as sedimentation, constitution, institution, intentionality, incorporation, extension, habit, bodily motility, spatiality, intersubjectivity, and body/mind dualism as they are presented through the work of founding figure of Phenomenology, Edmund Husserl, the Existential Phenomenology variation and focus on the body disclosed in the work of Maurice Merleau-Ponty, and the potential for intertwining Phenomenology and Cognitive Science in the more recent disclosure of Neurophenomenology.

# Philosophy of Mind [CG 606]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Cognitive Science and philosophy of mind: Epistemic and metaphysical issues of mind in Cognitive science Western theories of mind: Dualism, behaviorism, materialism, eliminativism functionalism, physicalism, phenomenology, representational theory of mind, modularity of mind and identity theory Neurobiological approaches to mind: Patricia Churchland arguments, the binding problem, the problem of Mary's Knowledge, Connectionism Computational approaches to mind: Searle's Chinese room argument, intentionality, the problem of intelligence and the representational nature of mind Indian theories of mind: Heterodox: Jainism and Buddhism; Orthodox: Samkhya, Nyaya and Vedanata

CG 606

#### Emotion and Cognition

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 1 | 4

 Course Content

# Emotion and Cognition [CG 607]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 1 | 4

 Historically, cognition and emotion were considered two independent processes that did not interact much with each other. Moreover, the study of emotions was considered subjective and therefore unscientific in a scientific world dominated by behaviourism. However, recent developments in cognitive neuro-sciences have enabled the study of emotions in a scientific manner and have increasingly shown evidence to its interconnectedness to cognition. What are the implications of such inter-connectedness? How do our feelings affect our thoughts, memory, decision making etc. and vice-versa? Topics will include: nature and measurement of emotions, categorical and dimensional approaches to affect, Affect cognition relations in specific cognitive processes like perception, attention, memory and judgement.

CG 611

#### Neural Plasticity

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Neural Plasticity [CG 612]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 The emergence of plasticity: experience-dependent plasticity in cats (monocular deprivation), Hubel & Weisel’s experiments, the concept of ‘critical period’, developmental plasticity and correlation with cognitive development

 Adult plasticity: plasticity following peripheral deafferentation in adults, Merzenich & Kaas’ experiments, limits and phases of adult plasticity, phantom limb phenomenon, peripheral vs CNS plasticity (stroke), motor plasticity and performance - expertise and time course of plasticity.

 Cross-modal plasticity: somatosensory plasticity following blindness, Molyneux question, plasticity in the newly sighted, visual cortex of deaf, rewiring of cortex in animal models (Pallas and Sur experiments)

 Neural mechanisms of plasticity: axonal sprouting, dendritic spine dynamics, synaptic plasticity ( LTD, LTP), Hebbian and homeostatic plasticity, the molecular and genetic basis of plasticity mechanisms

 Plasticity for cognitive enhancement: mechanisms for reintroducing critical period plasticity in animals, FastForWord - Merzenich and Paula Talal, brain training apps, stereopsis training for adult plasticity

CG 612

#### Analysis and Modelling of Locomotion

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Course Content

# Analysis and Modelling of Locomotion [CG 613]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Introduction to the basics of anatomy and physiology of locomotion, evaluations methods in clinics, and gait disorder; Spatio-temporal gait analysis. Walking phase detection, measurement of stride length, stride velocity, cadence, and other spatio-temporal parameters. Gait symmetry, gait variability, and gait coordination measurement. Clinical gait analysis. Practical examples to clinics, dual-task paradigm, and modelling relevant analogies of equivalents of locomotion; Kinematics approach for gait analysis, kinematics measurement, and motion capture. Stereo-photogrammetry, ultrasound, and magnetic motion capture. Accelerometers, gyroscopes, magnetometers and inertial-based motion capture systems, Lab vs. daily life monitoring, anatomical and functional calibration, joint kinematics, and application in orthopedics; Kinetics of locomotion. Forces and moment measurements- Forces transducers and force plates, pressure measuring systems and pressure insoles, combining kinetics with kinematics, energy, and power of body segment inverse dynamics, muscular activity. Application to knee adduction moment measurement; Neural basis of locomotion. Spinal circuitry underlying locomotion, the role of sensory information, modulation through descending systems, and cortical circuitry contributing to locomotion. Implication for the design of gait neuroprosthesis and exoskeletons; Models of the mechanics of biped locomotion. Inverted pendulum models. Spring-loaded inverse pendulum models. Links to robotics such as passive and dynamic walkers.

CG 613

#### Human Brain Mapping

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Human Brain Mapping [CG 614]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Principles of Brain Mapping: Localization of mental functions including perception, memory, attention and executive processes in the human brain and the study of their neural mechanisms; Mapping methods: Fundamentals and principles of mapping methods like functional Magnetic Resonance Imaging (fMRI), and Electroencephalography (EEG). fMRI – Basics of MR physics, image acquisition, acquisition protocols, basics of T1w/T2w/T2* imaging, understanding BOLD data and sources of noise, design of experiments suitable for BOLD imaging, analysis of BOLD task and rest data, visualization of BOLD images, creation of individualized and averaged brain atlases; EEG – basics of EEG, experiment design, properties of data, sources of noise, Event Related Potentials, signal processing of brain state, microstates analysis, principles behind concurrent EEG-fMRI. Brain connectivity: Understanding how brain function and connectivity are interrelated, brain connectome, diffusion weight imaging (DWI), fMRI connectivity, changes with various disorders, connectivity based machine learning models, graph-based network properties.

CH 302

#### Food Chemistry

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Food Chemistry [CH 401]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 1. Description of Core Constituents of Foods: Understanding of biomolecular composition of raw fruits, vegetables and meats. Alteration in chemical constituents during standard food preparation (cooking/processing) approaches. Changes in bonding, composition and activity of water during food preparation.
 2. Food Flavor, Color and Aroma: Molecular basis of flavor, aroma and color of foods; changes during standard food processing methods.
 3. Biochemical transformations underlying food processing; implications on flavor, color and aroma. Production of food products with corresponding enzymes such as: high-fructose corn syrup (a-amylase, glucose isomerase), Cheese and dairy products (chymosin, b-galactosidase), Bread making/baking (groups of enzymes), Malt brewing (a-amylase, protease), fruit juices (pectic enzyme). Immobilized enzymes for analytical processes, starch to fructose conversion. Pasteurization of milk.
 4. Additives, Contaminants and Adulterants: Molecular basis of additional ingredients that are intentional, incidental and/or hazardous additives. Introduction to chemical basis of preservative action. Established chemical measures of food safety.
 5. Analytical Methods and Approaches: for study of composition, color, texture, flavor and aroma. Detection and quantification of hazardous substances. Introduction to global regulatory framework of food safety. Detection of chemical adulterants and detect antibiotic residues in foods (IR spectrometer), Colorimetric detection of pesticides and food additives (UV-vis spectrometer), pesticide residue and veterinary drugs in foods (LC-Mass analysis).
 6. Hands-on Experiments: Adulteration in edible oil, added food colors/dyes and sugars in the food items and soft drinks can be tested /detected by simple demonstration based experiments. Students would be able to test the samples of food items/soft drink by simple instruments such as: polarimeter, chromameter and UV-visible spectrometer. The details are provided in Annexure II.

 Course Content

# Advance Organic Chemistry [CH 513]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Rearrangement reactions: Electrophilic (Beckman, Hofmann, Lossen, Curtius, Wolff, Schimidt, Baeyer- Villiger, Pinacol-pinacolone, Wagner-Meerwein etc.), Nucleophilic (benzilic acid, Favorski), radical rearrangement (Wittig, aza-Wittig). Photochemistry: General principles, cis-trans-Isomerazation, photoisomerization of 1,3-butadiene, photochemistry of carbonyl compounds, photochemistry of aromatic compounds, cycloaddition reactions, photochemical reactions of 1,4-butdienes.

CH 521

#### Inorganic Chemistry Laboratory

#### Credit L | T | P | C 0 | 0 | 3 | 2

 Course Content

# Inorganic Chemistry Laboratory [CH 522]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
0 | 0 | 3 | 2

 The Inorganic Chemistry Lab will have the following list of experiments: Synthesis of transition metal complexes: Objective is to learn synthetic skills to prepare coordination complexes of 3d metals [e.g. Ni(II) and Co(II)] from their metal salts. The experiment will also demonstrate the use of few topics “spectrochemical series” and “chelate effect” covered in theory course. Polarimetry: Experiment involves breakdown of disaccharide sugar (sucrose) into its components (glucose and fructose) and kinetic study of this process with the help of Polarimeter instrument. Optical rotation of organic substances (sugars) can be measured and in-turn the rate constant. Gravimetric analysis: Purpose is to learn basic principles of Gravimetry, an analytical method used for the quantitative estimation of analytes (metal present in the solution) by treating it with the reagents to give sparingly soluble products which can be precipitated and weighed. Complexometric titration: Basic principles of titrimetric method will be demonstrated and hardness of water can be calculated by the solutions of of Zn(II), Mg(II) salts and titrating them with EDTA (ethylenediamine tetraaceticacid), a chelating reagent. Recycling of aluminium: This “Green experiment” is used to demonstrate, how aluminium foils can be recycled by converting it to potash alum in the chemical laboratory. Estimation of phosphoric acid: Phosphoric acid is used as acidifier in “Cola drinks” and its amount can be determined by interesting chemical method using molybdenum complex and “Absorption spectrophotometer”. Back Titration: Direct titration of the aluminium ions in the solution is challenging, due to the slow formation of the metal complex with the chelating reagents and non-availability of suitable metal indicator. Thus, back titration of solution containing mixture of ions (Zn 2+ and Al 3+ ) with EDTA can be used effectively to determine the aluminium present in the solution. UV-visible Studies: Experiment includes measurement of optical density (OD) of the given set of metal complexes in solution, which can be correlated with their Crystal Field Stabilization Energy (CFSE in Octahedral geometry). Fluorescence Studies: This involves solution based emission studies of organic dyes prepared in the “Organic Chemistry Lab” and calculate the emission quantum yield with the help of a fluorescence standard. IR Spectral analysis of compounds: Experiment includes hands-on training of IR spectrometer and learn basic interpretation of IR spectra of metal complexes and selected organic compounds; which is a simple analytical tool to find out the functional groups present in the sample.

CH 522

#### Organic Chemistry Laboratory

#### Credit L | T | P | C 0 | 0 | 3 | 2

 Course Content

# Organic Chemistry Laboratory [CH 523]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
0 | 0 | 3 | 2

 Synthesis of dibenzyledeneacetone- Experimental demonstration of aldol condensation for the carbon-carbon bond forming reaction. Solvent extraction- Separation of an Acidic, a Basic and a Neutral Substance using solvent extraction will be performed. Interpretation of IR spectra of organic molecules- IR spectrum of the synthesized organic molecules will be recorded and the interpretation of the spectrum will be carried out to identify the organic molecules. Interpretation of NMR spectra of organic molecules- NMR spectrum of the synthesized organic molecules will be recorded and the interpretation of the spectrum will be carried out to identify the organic molecules.

CH 524

#### Physical Chemistry Laboratory

#### Credit L | T | P | C 0 | 0 | 3 | 2

 Course Content

# Physical Chemistry Laboratory [CH 525]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
0 | 0 | 3 | 2

 Acetic acid adsorption over charcoal-Testing the physisorption models. Thermodynamics of charge transfer complexes- Estimating parameters such as enthalpy and entropy from an equilibrium reaction. Elevation in boiling point to calculate the boiling point elevation constant- Verifying this physical phenomenon by using a number of solute and solvent. Calculating the distribution constant and/or equilibrium constant of I 2 in aqueous medium- An equilibrium reaction between potassium iodide and iodine will be used to estimate the parameters. Electron transfer studies in Fe 3+ /Fe 2+ system using electrochemical techniques- Several techniques such as cyclic voltammetry will be used to understand the electron transfer process. Recording and interpretation of powder XRD pattern of an oxide materials- Specific oxide sample will be used to record the x-ray diffraction and its interpretation will be taught. Estimating the density of a crystalline powder using x-ray diffraction method- Powder x-ray diffraction pattern will be recorded and the lattice parameter estimation will reveal the density. Sol-gel synthesis of SiO 2 aerogels- Sol-gel synthesis will be utilized to make few amorphous and crystalline material and characterize Temperature dependent adsorption of CO 2 over solid materials- A detector based technique will be used to quantify the adsorption of gases such as CO 2 on adsorbent materials. Biodiesel synthesis using soybean oil and its characterization using TGA- Base catalyzed reaction will be used to synthesize the biodiesel. Due to specific combustion behavior, biodiesel can be easily characterized by TGA. Thermal decomposition behavior of carbonates, nitrates and hydroxides using TGA- Thermal behavior of the mentioned materials will be used to understand their decomposition behavior by TGA technique.

CH 525

#### Group Theory & Spectroscopy

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Group Theory & Spectroscopy [CH 526]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Symmetry and group theory: Introduction to group theory, properties of a group, Symmetry elements and symmetry operations, Symmetry point groups; Matrix representation of groups, properties of matrix representations, reducible and irreducible representations, character tables, Orthogonality relationships in group theory; Application of group theory in spectroscopy: Basic description of light-matter interactions using time-dependent perturbation theory approach, transition probability and selection rules; Molecular vibrations, Simple harmonic oscillator model and quantum mechanical solution, application of group theory to molecular motions, IR and Raman spectroscopy; Electronic spectroscopy, Molecular orbitals, selection rules, Emission of light: spontaneous and stimulated emission. Various applications in lasers and in imaging.

CH 527

#### Transition Metal Chemistry

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Transition Metal Chemistry [CH 528]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Theories of Bonding in Coordination Chemistry (VBT, CFT), Metal d-orbital splitting in different geometries (using CFT) Jahn-Teller distortion with examples in different geometries. The Isomerism with examples in coordination complexes. Electronic Spectroscopy: Term symbols, Laporte and Spin transition rules. Orgel diagrams: Absorption spectra of transition metal complexes (3d series) Tanabe-Sugano Diagrams, Lanthanides and Actinides, Molecular magnetism Calculation of magnetic moments (μs, μJ): (in d-block, Lanthanides and Actinides elements), Effects of the structural geometry on magnetic moments.

CH 528

#### AI in Chemistry

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# AI in Chemistry [CH 529]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Fundamentals of machine learning for chemistry: supervised, unsupervised, and reinforcement learning with examples from molecular and reaction modeling; Chemical data representation and feature engineering: SMILES and molecular graphs, atomic and bond features, molecular descriptors and fingerprints using RDKit; Chemical informatics tools and databases: overview of key datasets (QM9, ZINC, PubChem) and open-source packages (scikit-learn, Chemprop, DeepChem); Machine learning for chemical property prediction: regression and classification models for thermochemical, electronic, and solubility data; model validation and benchmarking; Deep learning for molecular modeling: neural networks and graph neural networks for structure–property relationships; introduction to ML potentials; Generative and inverse design approaches in chemistry: overview of variational autoencoders, GANs, and diffusion models for molecular design

#### Chemistry of Natural Products

 Course Content

# Chemistry of Natural Products [CH 602]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This is an advanced course once the students are familiar with basic synthetic chemistry principles. Reactions of heterocyclic compounds (which form the basic skeleton of natural products) includes imidazoles, oxazoles, thiazoles, pyridazines, pyrimidines and pyrazines, terpenes, alkaloids, antibiotics, carbohydrates, vitamins, nucleic acids and proteins. This course will also offer biosynthetic pathway of some important natural products.

CH 616

#### Interpretative Organic Spectroscopy

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Interpretative Organic Spectroscopy [CH 622]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 UV–Visible Spectroscopy: Electromagnetic spectrum, basic principles, concept of chromophore, electronic transitions in organic compounds, Structure elucidation by UV-Vis and Woodward – Fisher rules; IR – Spectroscopy: Basic principles, IR as a tool for functional groups determination in organic compounds; NMR Spectroscopy: Basic principles. Introduction to NMR techniques – CW and FT NMR techniques, 1H NMR Spectral parameters – intensity, chemical shift, multiplicity, coupling constant; Analysis of first and second order spectra. Multinuclear NMR (with specific emphasis on 13C NMR): Proton coupled, off–resonance decoupled 13C NMR spectra. Structure determination by NMR: Assignment of chemical shifts, additively effect, characteristic chemical shifts of common organic compounds and functional groups, DEPT spectra. Introduction to multinuclear NMR of common hetero atoms present in organic compounds (N, F, O, P, D). 2 D NMR techniques Homo- & Hetero-COSY and NOESY spectra; Mass Spectroscopy: Basic principles, techniques of ion production and ion and daughter ions, molecular ion and isotope abundance, Meta-stable ions, Fragmentation pathways: Mc-Lafferty Rearrangement and fragmentation pattern of common chemical classes. Application of MASS for organic structure elucidation: determination of molecular formula and MASS spectra-structure correlation.

CH 633

#### Introduction to Molecular Dynamics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Course Content

# Introduction to Molecular Dynamics [CH 634]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

Provides an introduction to molecular modelling. Course covers the following topics. Empirical force field models: Molecular mechanics; Molecular Dynamics simulation method; Energy minimization methods for exploring the energy surface; Density Functional Theory. The course will introduce students to bio-molecular simulation programs CHARMM and NAMD. The Gaussian 09 package will be introduced to perform DFT calculations. Tutorial sessions will be included into the curriculum. The course requires a project to be completed for credits.

Guided introduction to force field development and molecular dynamics simulations using a project heavy module to enable students to setup simulations and execute the same.
 1) Empirical force fields
 • The energy components of the force field
 • Understanding the bonded and non-bonded terms and their contributions.
 • How to choose a force field
 • Additive vs Polarizable force fields.
 2) Parametrization protocol to develop a force field
 • How to develop consistent parameters
 • Parameter transfer vs Parameter development
 • What to target for parametrization
 • Density Functional Theory calculations
 • Crystallographic databases
 • Thermodynamic data
 • NMR observables.
 3) Energy minimization methods
 • Steepest descent method, Newton Raphson method and Adopted basis Newton Raphson method.
 4) Molecular Dynamics simulation methodology and protocol
 • Periodic boundary conditions – Implementation and the crystal cell types, cubic vs truncated octahedron.
 • 2D boundary simulations - Slab Boundaries
 • Ewald Summation
 • Non-bonded cutoff schemes and their implementations– Switch vs Shift
 • Temperature schemes – Langevin dynamics vs Lowe-Andersen dynamics
 • Pressure schemes – Berendsen vs Nose-Hoover Langevin piston
 5) Which ensembles to use NVT vs NPT vs NVE – Parameters defining the choice of the ensembles.

6) Long simulations vs enhanced sampling simulations
 • Micro-second simulations
 • Replica exchange simulations
 • Free energy perturbations schemes.

Research Tools:
 • CHARMM (Force Field Development)
 • NAMD (Production Simulations on CPUs)
 • GROMACS (Production Simulations on GPUs)
 • Gaussian 09 (Density Functional Theory Calculations)

The course contents described above will be taught using a research project. Topics will be discussed by implementing the same in the project. Research projects will be devised which to target a very specific system setup. The projects will be picked up with emphasis on bio-molecular and nanoscale simulations.

CH 635

#### Statistical Thermodynamics and its Applications in Chemistry

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Statistical Thermodynamics and its Applications in Chemistry [CH 636]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Module I: Statistical Viewpoint
 Thermodynamics postulates; conditions of equilibrium; reversible processes and maximum work postulate; Maxwell relation; review of probability theory; phase space; microstates and configurations; Boltzmann distribution law; significant configurations; physical interpretation of the distribution law; variation of W with E; approach to thermal equilibrium; the free particle and the ideal gas; concept of entropy

Module II: The Partition Function
 Definition of partition function; chemical equilibrium and partition function; formulation of partition function – multiple degrees of freedom, degeneracy, indistinguishable and multiple units; thermodynamic functions from partition function – energy, entropy, factorability and separability; Fermi-Dirac and Bose-Einstein distributions and their classical limit

Module III: Evaluations of Partition Functions
 Translational partition function – ideal monoatomic gas, Sackur-Tetrode equation; multitude of translational states, Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution; internal degrees of freedom – rotation, vibration, electronic excitation, potential energy function for a diatomic molecule; rigid-rotor harmonic-oscillator approximation; polyatomic gases; monoatomic crystals; molecular symmetry and relationship with partition function

Module IV: Statistical Mechanical Ensembles
 Ensemble concept – basic postulates, types; microcanonical ensemble; canonical ensemble; grand canonical ensemble; analytical descriptions of the ensembles; equivalence of ensembles; fluctuations of thermodynamic properties

Module V: Applications
 Intermolecular potential, Virial coefficients; heat capacities – Einstein solid, diatomic gases; the equilibrium state – dissociation of iodine, determinants of equilibrium; transition state theory – potential energy surface; distribution functions in classical monoatomic liquids

 Course Content

# Organic Electronics [CH 637]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C

 Molecular Crystals; Amorphous Molecular/Polymer Films; Electronic States of a Molecule; Atomic Orbitals to Molecular Orbitals; Orbitals to States; Transitions between States; Potential Energy Curve; Radiative Transitions: Absorption and Emission; Electronic Factor; Vibrational Factor; Spin Factor; Franck-Condon Factor and Energy Gap Law; Electronic Coupling; Accepting Modes, Promoting Modes, and the Isotope Rule; Basic Photophysical Parameters: Lifetimes and Quantum Yields; Molecular Excitations – Effects Due to Polarization, Statistical Averaging, Environment, and Couplings; Dimers and Excimers; Electronic Interaction in the Ground and Excited States; Förster and Dexter Energy Transfer; Creation of Charged Molecules by Injection, Absorption, and Doping; Charged Molecules in Disordered Films and Molecular Crystals; Electrical and Optical Gap; Amorphous Solids; Comparison between Inorganic and Organic Semiconductors; Charge Carriers and Excitons; Frenkel and Wannier Exciton; Injection and Extraction of Charge Carriers; Charge Transport in Organic Solids; Band Transport; Hopping Transport; Polaronic Transport; Disorder Controlled Transport; Trapping Effect; Impact of Charge Carrier Density and Morphology on Transport; Charge Carrier Recombination; Generation and Dissociation of Excitations; Diffusion of Excitons; Basics of Organic Light-Emitting Diodes; Device Structures; Fluorescent and Phosphorescent Emitters; Organic Solar Cells Based; Electron-Donors and Electron-Acceptor Materials; Bulk-Heterojunction Structure; Role of Morphology of the Active Layer; Operational Principle of Organic Field-Effect Transistors; Improving Charge Transport; Polarity; Ambipolar Transport.

CH 637

#### Electronic Structure Theory

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Electronic Structure Theory [CH 638]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Overview: Quantum theory and origin of electronic structure; many-electron problem; Born-Oppenheimer approximation; periodic solids and bands; uniform electron gas and simple metals. Basis sets, linear algebra, and secular equation; Slater and Gaussian type orbitals; Wave-function based approaches: Hartree, Hartree-Fock, self-consistent field method; correlation corrections; configuration interaction theory; coupled cluster methods; many-body perturbation theory; Density functional theory: DFT foundations; Thomas-Fermi-Dirac approximations; Hohenberg- Kohn Theorems; intricacies of DFT; Kohn Sham variational equations; time dependent DFT; local spin density approximation; GGA; LDA; solving Kohn-Sham equations; Numerical implementations: basis sets; atomic pseudopotentials; Brillouin zone sampling; iterative diagonalization methods; software packages; Ground state properties of materials: Hellmann-Feynman theorem; stress tensor; geometry optimizations; electronic band structures; Fermi surfaces; Electronic excitations: time-dependent density functional theory; Bethe-Salpeter equation; optical spectroscopy; Hands-on component: Predicting properties of matter from electronic structure.

 Course Content

# Bio-inorganic Chemistry [CH 640]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Biogeochemical cycles: Energy transduction between the biotic and abiotic world (Emphasis on the carbon, hydrogen, and nitrogen cycles); Transport and storage of metals in biology: Transferrin and Ferritin will be discussed in detail by using iron as a model system; Metals in medicine: Development and current usage of various metal-based anti-cancer agents and MRI contrast agents; Hydrolytic chemistry by metalloenzymes: Role of acid-base chemistry in biology.  L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 • CO₂ storage and conversion technologies (3)

# Non-linear Spectroscopy and its Applications [CH 642]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 • Time-dependent quantum mechanics (8 lectures): Schrödinger equation, time evolution Hamiltonian and operator, light-matter interaction, two-level system, time-dependent perturbation theory, Fermi’s Golden rule, Schrödinger and Heisenberg representations, Interaction picture.

 • Hand-on learning (4 classes): UV-Vis, fluorescence, FTIR spectroscopy, time-correlated single photon counting (TCSPC).a

 L | T | P | C

CL 327

#### Chemical Engineering Practice in Industry

#### Credit L | T | P | C 1.5 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Course Content

# Chemical Engineering Practice in Industry [CL 328]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 0 | 2

 The Chemical Engineering Practice course aims to bridge the gap between academic learning and industrial application, focusing on the practical aspects of Chemical Engineering concepts. The course will consist of lectures delivered by invited instructors who are experienced professionals from the Chemical Industry. The lectures will cover case studies and current industry standards on:
 • Synthesis and production of organic and inorganic chemicals
 • Optimization of separation processes in industrial settings
 • Challenges in scaling up reactions and optimizing reaction conditions for yield/selectivity
 • Safety as well as design of industrial reactors
 • Process optimization and troubleshooting using control systems
 • Application of Chemical Engineering concepts in food industry
 • Production of specialty chemicals, catalysts, high-value products (essential oils, perfumes, etc.)
 • Production methods, processing techniques, and quality control in polymer industry
 • Process development, scaling up, technology transfer, and compliance with regulatory standards in pharmaceutical industry
 • Case studies on application of Chemical Engineering beyond the Chemical Industry (for example, semiconductors, biomedical devices, automobiles, aerospace, defense, etc.) and related technical challenges.

 Course Content

# Introduction to Process Safety [CL 353]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Introduction to Process Safety: What is process safety? Difference between process safety and occupational safety, Importance in the chemical industry, Accident and loss statistics; learning from history, Brief on inherently safer design.

 Accident Investigation & Case Studies: Simple accident investigation process, Root cause thinking, Brief review of major industrial accidents (e.g., Bhopal, Texas City, Flixborough) and key lessons.

CL 426

#### Formulation Science and Engineering

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Formulation Science and Engineering [CL 427]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 The course is focused on liquid, semisolid (paste-like) formulations where one or more components are dispersed in a solvent. The course will be taught in the form of modules, each module focusing on a formulation with a specific application.
 Module-I: Introduction to formulations design. Examples of formulations.
 Module-II: Suspensions: Formulations of solid/liquid dispersions. Understanding of inter-particle interaction potential, stability, and potential stabilizer. Discussion on properties of dilute, semi-dilute, and concentrated suspensions.
 Module-III: Emulsions: Formulations of liquid/liquid dispersions. Synthesis and thermodynamics of emulsification. Introduction to nanoemulsions, microemulsions, Pickering, and multiple emulsions. Stabilization and destabilization of emulsions. Discussion on properties of dilute, semi-dilute, and concentrated emulsions.
 Module-IV: Foams: Formulations of air/liquid dispersants. Surface force theory. Surface viscosity and elasticity. Foam films, foam drainage, foam collapse
 Module-V: Evaluation techniques for formulations after dilution
 Module-VI: Evaluating formulations without dilution. Discussion on toxicity evaluation of formulations

CL 627

#### Liquid State Theory

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Liquid State Theory [CL 628]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Recap of statistical mechanics: Microstates and macrostates, canonical ensemble, isothermal-isobaric ensemble, grand canonical ensemble, introduction to the types of simulations. Functionals: Introduction to functionals and their differentiation, functional derivatives of the grand potential. Distribution functions: Introduction to Distribution functions, direct correlation functions, density response functions, Classical density functional theory. Diagrammatic methods: Diagrammatic expansions of direct correlation functions, Introduction to virial expansion of the equation of state. Integral equation theory: Yvon-Born-Green hierarchy, Ornstein-Zernike equation, hypernetted chain approximation, Percus-Yevick approximation, mean spherical approximation. Time-dependent correlations: Properties of time correlation functions, Langevin equation, linear response theory, time-dependent fluctuations, estimation of the macroscopic transport coefficients. Molecular liquids: Molecular pair distribution function, Site-site distribution functions, Reference Interaction Site Model (RISM) equations. Inhomogeneous liquids: Free energy functionals for liquids at interfaces, application to liquid-vapor interface, calculation of surface tension, density functional theory of wetting.

CL 628

#### Fundamentals of Aerosol Science

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Fundamentals of Aerosol Science [CL 629]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

The proposed course encompasses aerosols' properties, transport, and transformation that one would need a firm hold on to mitigate undesired aerosols and engineer desired aerosols or nanoparticles via the aerosol pathway.

The size range of particles for this course will be from a few nanometers to >20 microns.

The course content is divided into the following modules for ease of reference:

Physical and chemical characterization: size and shape, particle morphology, size distribution functions, moments of distribution function, fractals, self-preserving size distribution, common instrumentation

Transport and deposition: diffusion, convection, force migrations, thermophoresis, inertial Transport and deposition, impactors, gravitational settling, design and operating principle of aerosol instruments based on particle transport

Optical properties: light scattering by a single particle, Mie theory, Rayleigh scattering, aerosols impact on visibility, design and operating principle of aerosol instruments based on optical properties

Aerosol dynamics: coagulation (Brownian and force-field), collision frequency function, thermodynamic properties, Kelvin Relation, nucleation, gas to particle conversion, evaporation, condensation, general dynamic equation

CL 629

#### Catalyst Design for Heterogeneous Reactions

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Catalyst Design for Heterogeneous Reactions [CL 630]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 1. Introduction to heterogeneous catalysts: prominent industrial thermal catalysts; unconventional catalyst synthesis techniques – vacuum synthesis, autoclave methods, thermal methods
 2. Fundamentals of heterogeneous catalysis: turnover-rate; tools (mass spectrometry, gas chromatography, titration) and methods of catalytic measurements; transient and steady-state measurements; mass transfer limitations and catalyst loss
 3. Basics of chemical bonding and reaction barriers
 • Free energy/ scaling relationship
 4. Understanding elementary catalytic steps: pressure dependence; kinetic isotope measurements; steady-state isotopic transient kinetic analysis
 5. Catalyst design:
 • Can the ligand effects and ensemble effects be decoupled?
 • Ex situ tools
 • Composition, importance of particle and crystallite sizes, morphology, acidity/basicity (NH3 adsorption), heats of adsorption (TPD, isothermal calorimetry, differential scanning calorimetry)
 • Surface science studies of crystalline catalysts; Measure of reaction barriers
 • In situ tools
 • Importance and challenges, current status; time-resolved measurements and effective spectroscopic cell design
 • Spectroscopic technique selection rules; X-ray techniques, electron beam techniques, light techniques (IR, Raman, UV-vis) and other techniques
 • Operando measurements
 • Set up and limitations, structure-function relationships, compatibility with in situ spectroscopic techniques
 • Case-studies of in situ and operando measurements – strength and pitfalls

CL 630

#### Pharmaceutical Crystallization

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Pharmaceutical Crystallization [CL 631]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Basics of crystallization: Solubility, solid-liquid equilibrium, supersaturation, metastability, nucleation, crystal growth, crystal structure, crystal habit, population balance, control of particle size and morphology, application of crystallization in pharmaceuticals and foods

Processing and characterization of pharmaceutical crystals: Continuous crystallization, antisolvent crystallization, evaporative crystallization, reactive crsyatllization, filtration and washing, drying, morphology/habit control, milling of organic crystals, blending and granulation of pharmaceutical powders, Solid-state characterization of pharmaceutical crystals using X-ray diffraction, Differential Scanning Calorimetry, Thermogravimetric Analysis, Infrared and Raman Spectroscopy, microscopic techniques
