CL 631

#### Advanced Separation Processes

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Advanced Separation Processes [CL 632]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Fundamentals of Separation Processes: An overview of mass transfer fundamentals such as driving forces, and mass transfer coefficients, overview of basic separation processes (gas absorption, distillation, extraction etc.) and their limitations; Membrane Separation Processes: Membrane types, materials, synthesis, and characterization. Transport mechanisms (e.g., solution-diffusion model) and membrane modules, Pressure-driven processes such as microfiltration (MF), ultrafiltration (UF), nanofiltration (NF), and reverse osmosis (RO), Concentration polarization and membrane fouling, Other membrane techniques like dialysis, electrodialysis, gas separation, and pervaporation; Adsorption and Chromatography: Adsorption equilibria (e.g., Langmuir and Freundlich isotherms), Adsorbents and ion exchangers, Chromatographic techniques, retention theory, and design, Advanced methods like pressure swing adsorption (PSA), temperature swing adsorption (TSA), and simulated moving bed (SMB) chromatography; Supercritical Fluid Processes (SFE): Properties of supercritical fluids (typically supercritical CO2) and phase equilibria, Working principle, advantages over conventional solvents, and industrial applications (e.g., extraction of essential oils from seeds, spices and flowers); Advanced and Hybrid Distillation Techniques: Multicomponent distillation, Enhanced distillation methods, including azeotropic and extractive distillation. Reactive and catalytic distillation, combining reaction and separation in a single unit for process intensification, Molecular distillation and short-path distillation for processing heat-sensitive materials. Pressure swing distillation; Advanced Crystallization: Basics of crystallization, principles of melt crystallization and fractional crystallization, Techniques like zone melting and freeze crystallization, Supercritical Fluid Crystallization processes

CL 632

#### Applications of AI in ChE

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Applications of AI in ChE [CL 633]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Foundations of AI and Chemical Engineering (Overview and AIs transformative role in chemical engineering, Understanding AI paradigms: machine learning, deep learning, and hybrid approaches, Practical applications including process control, optimization, and materials discovery); Data Handling and Preparation (Nature of chemical engineering data: process measurements, molecular data, and time series, Techniques for data preprocessing—cleaning, normalization, and feature extraction, Exploratory data analysis and visualization tools to reveal underlying patterns); Classical Machine Learning Methods (Key supervised learning algorithms: linear and nonlinear regression, classification (Support Vector Machines (SVM), decision trees, ensembles), Model selection strategies, validation techniques, and performance measurement, Applications: Prediction of thermophysical properties of solutions); Unsupervised Learning and Dimensionality Reduction (Clustering techniques—k-means, hierarchical, density-based clustering of applications with noise (DBSCAN)—and their uses in process monitoring, Dimensionality reduction methods such as principal component analysis (PCA) and t-distributed stochastic neighbour embedding (t-SNE) for handling complex chemical data, Applications in anomaly detection and development of soft sensors for chemical reactors); Deep Learning in Chemical Engineering (Introduction to neural network architectures including Multilayer Perceptrons (MLPs), Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), and Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs), Tasks such as reaction prediction, fault detection, and image analysis in chemical processes, Addressing overfitting, generalization, and interpreting deep learning models); Integrated Modelling, Physics-Informed Machine Learning, and Optimization (Hybrid Modelling: Combining mechanistic first-principles models with data-driven AI to create robust, efficient process simulators and surrogate models, Physics-Informed Machine Learning (PIML): Embedding physical laws, constraints, governing differential equations directly into machine learning models, physics-based feature engineering, custom loss functions incorporating PDE residuals, neural network architectures constrained by process physics, Applications focus on modelling complex multi-physics phenomena in chemical reactors, including momentum, heat, and mass transfer, nonlinear chemical kinetics, Optimization and Control: Implementing AI for real-time process optimization, predictive maintenance, anomaly detection, and adaptive control strategies. Use of model predictive control (MPC) frameworks and reinforcement learning for scheduling and decision-making.

CL 633

#### Statistical Mechanics of Liquids

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Statistical Mechanics of Liquids [CL 634]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Recap of statistical mechanics: Microstates and macrostates, canonical ensemble, isothermal-isobaric ensemble, grand canonical ensemble, introduction to the types of simulations; Functionals: Introduction to functionals and their differentiation, functional derivatives of the grand potential; Distribution functions: Introduction to Distribution functions, direct correlation functions, density response functions, Classical density functional theory; Diagrammatic methods: Diagrammatic expansions of direct correlation functions, Introduction to virial expansion of the equation of state; Integral equation theory: Yvon-Born-Green hierarchy, Ornstein-Zernike equation, hypernetted chain approximation, Percus-Yevick approximation, mean spherical approximation; Time-dependent correlations: Properties of time correlation functions, Langevin equation, linear response theory, time-dependent fluctuations, estimation of the macroscopic transport coefficients; Experimental estimation of distribution functions; Applications of machine learning.

#### Integrated Principles of Chemical Engineering

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Integrated Principles of Chemical Engineering [CL 635]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction to Chemical Engineering – Overview of chemical engineering and its role in chemical, biological, and materials processes, conservation principles (mass and energy balances), and unit operations and unit processes; Thermodynamics – Thermodynamic systems and properties, internal energy, First Law of Thermodynamics, simple energy balances for closed systems, enthalpy and heat capacity, ideal gas behavior, sensible and latent heat, Second Law of Thermodynamics; Fluid Mechanics – Fluid properties and viscosity, hydrostatic pressure, laminar and turbulent flow, Reynolds number and flow regimes, Bernoulli’s equation, Friction factor and frictional losses; Heat Transfer – Modes of heat transfer (conduction, convection), Fourier’s law of heat conduction, Newton’s law of cooling, thermal resistance concept, and overall heat transfer coefficient; Chemical Reaction Engineering – Material balances with chemical reactions, reaction stoichiometry, basic reaction rate concepts, ideal reactors including batch reactor, continuous stirred tank reactor (CSTR), Plug flow reactors (PFR) and comparison of reactor types; Mass Transfer – Molecular diffusion, Fick’s law of diffusion, diffusion coefficient, mass transfer coefficients, and basic concepts of gas absorption and distillation; Process Control – Basic dynamic behavior of process systems, response of first-order systems, block diagram representation of process systems, closed-loop control, and proportional–integral–derivative (PID) control concepts.

# Computer Networks [CS 331]

 L | T | P | C

Socket Interface and Socket programming; Advanced topics and recent trends in Computer Networks and Future Internet: Information Centric networking, Software Defined Networking (SDN), Network Function Virtualization (NFV), Internet of Things (IoT), Docker and Container Networking (CNIs), Programmable switches, OVS, and SmartNICs; Miscellaneous: Introduction to Datacenter Networks, Different RFCs; Case Studies: Linux/Unix TCP IP Stack; Network Simulators: NS-3, and Mininet; Optional: NS-2, OMNet,

 Course Content

# Ethics of AI [CS 333]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Introduction to AI Ethics and Accountability
 - Discuss the critical role ethics play in AI research and deployment, emphasizing the potential societal impacts and the necessity for responsible innovation to prevent harm and promote trust.
 -Identify stakeholders responsible for AI outcomes, including developers, organizations, and policymakers, and discuss the allocation of accountability.
Algorithmic Bias and Fairness
 - Introduction: Case studies on biased AI systems and explore sources of bias.
 - Fairness Definitions: Sufficiency, Separation and Calibration and their relationship with each other and accuracy
 - Fairness Approaches: Explore one in-processing, pre-processing and post-processing approach with
 application to real-world dataset for mitigating bias
Privacy
 - Case studies on Data Privacy
 - Reconstruction and Membership Inference Attacks
 - Introduction to Differential Privacy
 - Differentially Private - Stochastic Gradient Descent
Interpretability
 - Motivation and Goals for Interpretability
 - Overview of Model Agnostic Methods
 - Overview of Model Specific Methods.
 - Neural network interpretation
Global Perspectives and Regulations
 - International policies and guidelines on AI ethics.
 - Comparative analysis of AI regulations across countries.
 - The role of organizations in setting ethical standards.

#### Distributed Systems and Cloud Computing

 Course Content

 L | T | P | C

 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 ● Fundamentals: Definition of Turing Machines, Time and Space, Diagonalization, Hierarchy Theorems.
 ● Poly-time (P), Nondeterministic poly-time (NP), and completeness: Definitions of P and NP, Turing reductions and many-one reductions, NP-completeness of Satisfiability (SAT).
 ● Space complexity: Logspace (L) and Nondeterministic Logspace (NL), Savitch’s Theorem, logspace reductions, NL-completeness of directed graph reachability, NL=coNL (coNL indicating complement of NL), PSPACE (polynomial space).
 ● Randomness and Interaction: Bounded-error probabilistic polynomial-time (BPP) and randomized-polynomial time (RP), Polynomial Identity Testing (PIT), Valiant-Vazirani Theorem, Polynomial hierarchy (PH) and its characterizations, PSPACE (polynomial space), PSPACE-completeness of Quantified Boolean Formulas (QBF), interactive proofs (IP), Arthur-Merlin classes, IP = PSPACE.
 ● Non-uniformity: Boolean circuits, Nick’s class (NC), Matching in Randomized NC (RNC), Karp-Lipton Theorem.
 ● Cryptography and Complexity: One-way functions and Pseudorandom generators.
 ● Counting: #P, Toda’s theorem, The permanent
 ● Circuits: Alternating circuits of constant depth (AC0), Alternating circuits with counting (ACC0), Threshold circuits (TC0), Nick’s class level 1 (NC1) etc., AC0 lower bounds.
 ● Branching programs: Barrington’s Theorem, Monoid programs.
 ● Exact complexity: Exponential-time hypothesis (ETH) and Strong exponential-time hypothesis (SETH), sparsification lemma.
 ● Randomness vs Hardness: Derandomization implies circuit lower bounds.

CS 617

#### Theoretical Foundations of Machine Learning

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Theoretical Foundations of Machine Learning [CS 618]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Concentration of measure – various concentration inequalities; PAC learning, VC dimension, Rademacher complexity, generalization bounds, sample complexity. Generalization in deep learning setting – double descent; Optimization – random projection, randomized numerical linear algebra and its applications in efficient optimization (e.g. regression, matrix factorization, second order descent); Beyond the worst-case analysis of machine learning algorithms – algorithms for non-negative matrix factorization, tensor decomposition, sparse recovery, dictionary learning; Kernels and their applications in ML --- kernel trick, Representer theorem, Support vector machine with kernels, Green’s theorem, Mercer’s theorem, Bochner’s theorem; learning kernels; Subset selection and its variants --- using submodularity, using importance sampling (coresets). Applications in data valuation, continual learning, and in optimization; Introduction to Gaussian Process (GP), neural network-GP equivalence, neural tangent kernel; Learning in stochastic settings -- Introduction to online learning and multi-armed bandits; Gittin’s index, Thomson sampling, adversarial bandits, contextual bandits.

CS 618

#### CS Theory Toolkit

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# CS Theory Toolkit [CS 619]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Logic: Propositions, connectives, quantifiers, rules of inference,
 induction, strong induction, structural induction.

Combinatorics: Definition of graphs and various structures in graphs. Koenig, Hall, and
 Menger theorems. The TONCAS (The obvious necessary conditions are also sufficient)
 principle, Common proof techniques (structural induction, attacking minimal counterexamples etc.) for graph theory, algebraic techniques, algorithmic proofs (Havel-Hakimi
 theorem, Eulerian circuits, Pruefer codes). Advanced Topic: Parameterized algorithms:
 Branching algorithms and proof by cases, refining cases to increase efficiency, tree
 decompositions and dynamic programming (Independent set), algebraic techniques for
 exact algorithms (inclusion-exclusion)

Probability theory: Review of basics, Expectation and variance, linearity of expectation,
 concentration bounds, derandomization, probabilistic methods in proofs. Advanced topic: A
 randomized algorithm for perfect matching.\

Computational models: What is computation?, motivation and definition of RAM (Random
 access machine) model, Algorithmic correctness and worst-case complexity. lower bounds
 vs upper bounds, reductions, Satisfiability and reductions to Satisfiability (Hamiltonian Cycle
 to Satisfiability, Vertex Cover to Satisfiability), hardness and completeness via reductions.
 identifying the right reductions (many-one, Turing, logspace etc.), gadget constructions (one
 or two advanced examples).

Advanced topic: Boolean circuits, constant-depth circuit
 constructions, circuit reductions

CS 620

#### Ethics of AI

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Ethics of AI [CS 621]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Algorithmic Fairness and Accountability, where students study operationalized fairness criteria, constrained optimization for fair models, and the socio-technical dimensions of bias through case studies in vision and NLP; the Interpretability and Explainability module introduces inherently interpretable models, post-hoc explanations (LIME, SHAP, Integrated Gradients, SmoothGrad), counterfactual and concept-based explanations (TCAV, Network Dissection), and connects interpretability with fairness; Mechanistic Interpretability and Representation Engineering delves into circuits, compiled transformers, and emergent reasoning in large models (e.g., Chain-of-Thought prompting, interpretability in diffusion and vision-language models); Robustness and Adversarial ML explores vulnerabilities in AI systems, the history and theory of adversarial examples, adversarial training, robustness–accuracy trade-offs, and the security of large models under adversarial or distributional shifts; Privacy and Security in AI explores data protection, membership inference, reconstruction attacks, and the theoretical and practical aspects of Differential Privacy and DP-SGD; Ethics of Large-Scale AI Systems examines the societal, legal, and governance implications of LLMs and generative models, including transparency, accountability, and global regulatory trends.

CS 621

#### Combinatorial Game Theory

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Combinatorial Game Theory [CS 622]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction to Combinatorial Games: Game trees, Zermelo’s Theorem, foundations of CGT (finite, turn-based, 2 player, perfect information games) strategy stealing arguments, structural game analysis. Positions and outcome classes, sums of positions, equivalence and canonical forms. Normal and Misère play; Impartial Games: Nim and variants, Sprague–Grundy Theorem, MEX computation of Grundy values; Partizan Games: Hackenbush, dyadic rational values, simplicity principle, canonical forms. Construction of Surreal numbers from games, arithmetic, ordering, number–game correspondence; Extensions: The relaxation of axioms of combinatorial game theory to include a wider class of games. This includes multiplayer, hidden information and loopy games. We will look at specific game analyses here; Advanced topics: A survey of computational complexity and reductions, algorithmic results, game equivalences and temperature theory.

CS 622

#### Algebraic Complexity Theory

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Algebraic Complexity Theory [CS 623]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Review: fields, rings, polynomials, degree; Computational aspects: Polynomial families, algebraic circuits, formulas, size and depth; Homogenization lemma; Strassen's division elimination; Baur-Strassen theorem; The class VP (Valiant's Poly-size Circuits) and VF (Valiant's poly-size Formulas); p-projections; Determinant and the class VBP (Valiant's poly-size Branching Programs); Mahajan-Vinay ABP (algebraic branching program) for determinant; The class VNP (Valiant's Non-deterministic Poly-size); VNP-completeness of permanent; VNP in terms of VF; Determinantal complexity lower-bound for the permanent; VF-complete polynomials: Ben-Or and Cleve's construction, Narayana's cows polynomials; Monotone computation: monotone ABP for permanent; monotone circuit, ABP, and formula lower bounds; Multi-linear computation: lower bounds for multi-linear formulas computing the determinant; Border complexity: approximate width-2 algebraic branching programs and VF; Computational power of constant-depth circuits.

#### Data Center Systems Engineering

 Course Content

# Data Center Systems Engineering [CS 625]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 1.5 | 4

 Data centers as engineered systems (facility + IT + operations) and lifecycle; standards landscape and terminology (vendor-neutral); hyperscale vs enterprise vs colocation vs edge operating models; cloud-native platforms (virtualization, containers, microservices) and east-west networking fabrics; availability engineering: redundancy vs maintainability, failure domains, blast radius; observability as architecture (metrics/logs/traces), SLO/SLI mindset, actionable alerting and runbooks; performance and tail latency (p95/p99), capacity planning and saturation signals; security and auditability basics (identity, keys/HSM concepts, change control); AI-driven data centers: accelerator clusters, high rack-power density, heat removal constraints (airflow/containment and liquid-cooling awareness), and tighter facility-IT coupling; capstone: teams produce a reference design and an operational evidence pack (architecture diagrams, monitoring plan, drills, postmortems).

CS 625

#### Technopreneurship: Product Proposition

#### Credit L | T | P | C 2 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Course Content

# Technopreneurship: Product Proposition [DES 201]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
2 | 0 | 3 | 4

 The course intends to promote contextual innovation that is feasible and marketable. The students will get an opportunity to apply the theoretical knowledge of engineering to design a solution that works. The course structure and pedagogy will require creation of functional teams within students, simulating professional practice
 This course also aims to integrate entrepreneurial activity with the academic curriculum and promote innovations that are technologically strong.
 Design process to be central to the stage wise progress; cyclic iterative development will be integral to development of the product solution. Various tools and methods to find a focused issue and lead towards an effective solution will be employed suitably.
 Topics covered
 ▣ Introduction to business innovations
 - Drivers of successful innovations (usability, desirability and feasibility)
 - Opportunities for business innovations (organization structure, product offerings, customer experience) and their intersection with product innovation
 ▣ Define the project brief: opportunities for product innovation
 - Defining a need statement that leads to the investigation in target market segmentation - User profile: Investigate and define demographics of the target user as applicable.
 - Environment/ context: establishing the seasonal, geographical, social and environmental needs etc. as applicable
 - Prior art and/or existing solutions/trend analysis in the market and potential gap areas
 ▣ Concept development: iterative, cyclic design process
 - Application of design thinking tools such as mind maps, fishbone diagrams to be used for data mapping and analysis
 - Ideation (multiple ideas based on the opportunities): use of design methods such as Brainstorming/ SCAMPER / Synectics for ideation.
 - Consolidation of concepts using progressive sketches (schematic >> product concept) and low fidelity mock-ups using materials available around
 ▣ Product proposition
 - Consolidated proposition with user, context and design opportunities
 - Medium fidelity prototype(s) as a proof of concept: the prototype(s) should explain the working of the product and interaction with the user (combined or separate as required)

DES 302

#### Technopreneurship: Product Development + Business Strategy

#### Credit L | T | P | C 2 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Course Content

# Technopreneurship: Product Development + Business Strategy [DES 303]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
2 | 0 | 3 | 4

The course will involve a major project; where the students work parallely on multiple fronts to make the product solution ready for business incubation. Introductory sessions for each topic and mentoring will be provided to the students. The project is driven by student teams; seeking mentorship as they face roadblocks. Timely evaluations and feedback from a curated panel of members from the industry and business experts are integrated in the structure.

They will make extensive use of design as a strategic problem solving process that leads to better quality of life through innovative products, systems, services and experiences.
 Topics/activities covered:

• Developing high fidelity prototype (using in-house IITGN facilities and/or vendors and external mentorship)
 - Understanding criticalities of design detailing for materials and processes, assembly
 - Learning to plan for fabrication and assembly, optimizing the resources (material, time, human)
 - Material procurement, communication with vendor and logistics

• Protecting the intellectual property (as and if applicable)
 - Understanding the need and process of protecting intellectual property (Copyright / Patent/ Registration etc)
 - Scrutinizing which method and/or process will be most appropriate for the proposed concept

• Chalking out a business strategy: keeping the product solution as the focus
 - Vision for the near future such as identifying the target market size
 - Understanding the basics of production management, decisions on volume of production, projecting the resources required

• Customer feedback, validation from subject experts (adapted from user experience design)
 - Testing how the intended users interact with the product; modifications / demonstrations required for a smooth barrier free interaction.
 - Subject experts in fields such as medical experts, manufacturers,policy makers or NGO workers etc. as applicable for implementation of product solution.

• Understanding the basics of production and management
 - Making the participants ready for pilot production;decisions on volume of production, projecting the material resources and infrastructure required.
 - Inventory management and logistics required in the near future (as applicable to the product solution only)

• Registration of the business venture
 - Understanding business structures like Proprietorship, Partnership, Company (Private company or Public company), etc.
 - Legal and financial aspects of each of these structures
 - A brief introduction to statutory requirements

• Financial plan and resource management: for first two years of product venture management
 - Introduction to return on investment, break even point
 - How to come with a financial equation for a financial quarter or a fiscal
 - An understanding for capital vs operational funds required for the venture

• Presentation and representation of the project:
 - How to pitch the solution; various formats suitable for particular audiences
 - Media for verbal and nonverbal communication
 - Representation of technical data/ product details
 - Packaging the solution in a marketable format: usability, product handling, introduction to branding and packaging

DES 602

#### Fundamentals of 3D Modeling and Animation

#### Credit L | T | P | C 1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Course Content

# Fundamentals of 3D Modeling and Animation [DES 603]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 The course follows a spiral learning structure where core concepts—form, light, composition, and motion—are introduced through simple analog
 experiences and revisited in digital form with increasing complexity. Each spiral loop integrates both tactile and digital modes of learning, allowing
 students to first explore ideas through drawing, paper modeling, origami, or shadow play, and then translate those understandings into 3D modeling
 and animation using latest tools such as Maya, 3DS Max, and Blender.
 Introduction to form and space through sketching and observation of basic 3D shapes.
 Exploration of structure by constructing geometric forms using folds and surfaces.
 Understanding 3D environments, navigation, and creating simple shapes in a virtual space.
 Developing complex forms using transformations, editing techniques, and modifiers.
 Studying the behavior of light and shadow through observation and model interaction.
 Applying lighting techniques to create depth, focus, and contrast in modeled scenes.
 Composing visual scenes by arranging elements with attention to balance and hierarchy.
 Using viewpoint and framing tools to guide visual storytelling within the scene.
 Exploring surface qualities through materials, colors, and textures.
 Investigating the principles of motion through simple sequencing and visual timing.
 Animating objects with transformations to convey movement and rhythm.
 Final project development, rendering, and presentation of animated scenes.

EE 431

#### Analysis and Synthesis of Linear Circuits

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Analysis and Synthesis of Linear Circuits [EE 432]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

Module 1: Introduction to circuit analysis techniques:
 Motivation and Basics; Importance of circuit theory, practical applications in Electrical/Electronic systems, review of circuit elements, circuit models and network theorems, Laplace transforms, transfer functions, Topological description of network; Network variables; Source transformation technique; node-voltage analysis; mesh-current Analysis.

Module 2: m-port Network
 Tellegen's Theorem; Passive and Lossless Network; Z-parameter, Y-parameter, Transmission matrix and h parameters; network Interconnection; Characteristic impedance and propagation function.

Module 3: Transient and steady state analysis
 Transient response in RL, RC and RLC circuits; network equations and solutions using Laplace transform, initial conditions. Fourier analysis for periodic signals; Fourier transform, Steady- state response - amplitude and phase spectra, resonance.

Module 4: Network Functions
 Driving point function, transfer function, concepts of poles and zeros, Bode plots; Impulse response and convolution.

Module 5: Graph theory
 Fundamental theorem of graph theory; loop analysis; two basic facts of loop analysis, loop analysis for linear time-invariant networks, properties of the loop impedance matrix, cut-set analysis; cut-set analysis for linear time-invariant networks, properties of the cut-set admittance matrix.

Module 6: Passive Network Synthesis
 Elements of realizability theory; causality and stability, Hurwitz polynomials, positive real functions, elementary synthesis procedures, properties of L-C immittance functions, synthesis of L-C driving point immittances, synthesis of R-C impedances or R-L admittances, properties of R-L Impedance and R-C admittances. Poles and zeros, Foster‘s reactance theorem, Foster and Cauer forms of LC Networks, Foster and Cauer Canonic forms. Synthesis of RLC networks

Module 7: Filters
 Maximally flat low-pass filter approximation, transient response of low pass filter, synthesis of low pass filters, magnitude and frequency normalization, Frequency Transformations.

EE 432

#### Principles of Digital Communications

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Principles of Digital Communications [EE 433]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Optimum detection and matched filtering in AWGN, Inter-symbol interference (ISI), Nyquist criterion, and pulse shaping, Wireless channel models: flat and frequency-selective fading, Modulation performance in AWGN and fading channels; Motivation and coding gain, Linear block codes: Hamming codes and cyclic redundancy checks (CRC), Convolutional codes: encoding, trellis representation, and Viterbi decoding (hard decision); Turbo coding principles: concatenation, interleaving, puncturing, constraint length, and free distance, Iterative and soft-decision decoding (BCJR, log-MAP), LDPC code construction, sparse parity-check matrices, and belief-propagation decoding, Coding gain–complexity trade-offs and examples from modern standards (LTE, Wi-Fi, 5G); Symbol-timing recovery: early–late gate and Gardner algorithms, Carrier-frequency offset and phase recovery (PLL concepts, differential detection), Sensitivity of synchronization to SNR, multipath, and Doppler effects; Impact of multipath and ISI on detection, Pilot-aided channel estimation: correlation, least-squares (LS), and minimum mean-square-error (MMSE) estimators, Linear equalizers: Zero-Forcing (ZF) and MMSE, Nonlinear equalization: decision-feedback equalizer (DFE); Effects of imperfect synchronization and channel estimation, Error propagation in equalizers; performance–complexity trade-offs, System-level integration of coding, synchronization, channel estimation, and equalization in a complete single-carrier link, Case studies from LTE, NR, and Wi-Fi receivers.

EE 653

#### Carrier Transport in Advanced Semiconductor Devices

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Carrier Transport in Advanced Semiconductor Devices [EE 654]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction: Advancements in MOSFET scaling: bulk MOSFETs, thin-body SOI MOSFETs, multi-gate FETs, nanosheet FETs, 2D material FETs; Review of classical carrier transport: drift-diffusion and continuity equations, Einstein relation, effective mass, density-of-states, Fermi-Dirac statistics, classical size effects; Limitations of drift-diffusion approach in describing modern MOSFETs: quantum confinement effects, Review of Schr
 odinger equation and its solutions.
 Band-structure modeling beyond effective-mass approximation: Review of electronic band-structures (E-k dispersion); Full band-structure from Density-Functional-Theory (DFT); Characterization of band structures using effective mass, density-of-states, and electronic bandgap; Band-structure modeling approaches: effective-mass based Hamiltonian, k.p approach, tight-binding Hamiltonian, and Wannier function approach.
 Semi-classical carrier transport in semiconductors: Introduction to the probability current, Fermi’s golden rule, Carrier scattering mechanisms in semiconductors, Electron-phonon interactions, The relaxation time approximation, The Boltzmann Transport Equation (BTE), Validity of the relaxation time approximation, Limitations of semi-classical transport.
 Carrier transport in nanostructures: Atomic-size conduction channels; Landauer formula; Conductance quantization; General Landauer–Büttiker approach: transmission function, scattering matrix, recursive Green’s function; Open and closed system description using Non-Equilibrium Green’s Function (NEGF): channel Hamiltonian, electrode self-energies, retarded, advanced, lesser and greater Green’s functions, spectral function, charge density matrix; Transmission function and current.
 Applications of quantum transport in advanced nanoelectronics devices: High mobility and short-channel MOSFETs; Tunneling field-effect transistors (TFETs); Transport in low-dimensional materials, e.g., two-dimensional materials; Quantum computing devices; Molecular devices.

EE 654

#### Heterostructure Devices

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Heterostructure Devices [EE 655]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Module 1: Physics of Heterojunctions
 Submodules: Overview of Metal-Semiconductor (MS) junctions: Ideal MS contacts, Schottky diode, the origin of interface/surface states, practical considerations; Overview of Heterojunctions: Materials consideration, semiconductor alloys, lattice-matched systems, energy band-diagrams, types of heterojunctions (type-I, type-II, type-III); Effects of quantum confinement; Hot electrons and Ballistic electrons in heterostructures; Formation of two-dimensional electron gas (2DEG); Quantum wells, Superlattices.

Module 2: Heterojunction Bipolar Transistors (HBTs)
 Submodules: Review of homojunction bipolar junction transistors (BJTs): overview of BJTs, IV characteristics, advantages and disadvantages; Advantages of heterojunction over BJT device performance, High-frequency performance; Abrupt junction HBTs; Graded junction HBTs; Graded base HBTs; Double heterojunction HBTs, GaAs/InP/Si HBTs, Physical characterization of HBTs, Thermal effects, Performance metrics: transition frequency, maximum frequency, Electrical characterization of HBTs.

Module 3: High electron mobility transistors (HEMTs)
 Submodules: Modulation-doped field-effect transistor (MODFET): device structure, device physics, electrical characteristics, the use of III-V materials; Charge control of 2DEG; Gallium Arsenide (GaAs) based HEMTs; Indium Phosphide (InP) based HEMTs; Gallium nitride (GaN) based HEMTs: polarization in GaN material, AlGaN/GaN HEMTs, Polarization Effects in AlGaN/GaN HEMTs, current collapse, fabrication, and challenges.

 Module 4: Optical sources and detectors
 Submodules: Basics of sources/detectors; Device structure and materials involved; Figure of merits; Quantum dots based infrared photodetector (QDIP): III-V material based QDIPs, Effect of variabilities on performance parameters; QD based Lasers: working principle, basic characteristics of lasers, different types of lasers; Market demand; Technology trends.

Module 5: Van der Waals (vdW) heterostructures
 Submodules: Two-dimensional materials and vdW heterostructures: Properties, growth methods, physical characterization, advantages, and applications; Graphene-based vdW heterostructures: Atomic and electronic structure, device applications such as vertical tunnelling based devices; Advantages of transition metal dichalcogenides (TMD) vdW heterostructures based on MoS2, WS2, MoSe2, WSe2, etc., Electrical and optoelectronic properties, TMD heterostructures with twist angle.

EE 660

#### Fundamentals of Nanoelectronic Devices

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Fundamentals of Nanoelectronic Devices [EE 661]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Introduction: Advancements in MOSFET scaling: bulk MOSFETs, thin-body SOI MOSFETs, multi-gate FETs, nanosheet FETs, 2D material FETs; Review of classical carrier transport: drift-diffusion and continuity equations, Einstein relation, effective mass, density-of-states, Fermi-Dirac statistics, classical size effects; Limitations of drift-diffusion approach in describing modern MOSFETs.

 Review of quantum mechanics: Wave-particle duality, Wavefunction, Real and k-space representation, Electron as a wavepacket, Schro ̈dinger’s wave equation, Time-independent Schro ̈dinger equation, Solution for a free particle, reiterating concept of effective mass, Solution for bound electrons in 1D, introduction to quantum confinement and tunneling, Solutions for different electron barrier shapes commonly found in MOSFETs.

Electron density in nanoscale MOSFETs: Dispersion (E-k) relation and charge density in k-space: 1D (quantum wire), 2D (quantum well) and 3D (bulk MOSFET); Sub-bands in nanosheet FETs, Mobile charge below and above threshold voltage with Quantum Mechanical (QM) effects, Surface and volume inversion, 2D-MOSFET electrostatics and charge modeling.

Electron transport in nanoscale MOSFETs: Current increase with channel length scaling: Limit of conductance, Diffusive to Ballistic conduction, Landauer formula, Ballistic limit for nanoscale transistor; Quasi-Ballistic Transport: Ballistic to diffusive transition, Mean Free Path, Carrier scattering mechanisms in semiconductors, Mobility modelling; Current-voltage characteristics of nanoscale MOSFETs.

Connecting nanoscale Device Physics to Circuit Design: Overview of a PDK: Core Device Model, Example of modeling QM effects in BSIM-CMG, Layout dependent parasitic effects; Impact of QM effects on basic analog and digital circuit figure-of-merits.

EE 662

#### Advanced Wireless Communications

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Advanced Wireless Communications [EE 663]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Introduction – motivation, types of wireless networks, applications and design challenges in modern wireless systems;

 Wireless propagation – Free-space path loss, empirical pathloss models, shadowing, multipath fading, Doppler shift, coherence time and bandwidth, WSSUS model, stochastic fading models;

 MIMO wireless channel modeling – i.i.d. and Kronecker-based spatial channel models, spatial correlation, geometry-based models, narrowband and block-fading assumptions;

 Multi-antenna techniques – Diversity (Alamouti, STBC generalizations), Spatial Multiplexing, linear precoding (SVD, ZF, MMSE), ergodic and outage capacity, AoA estimation (MUSIC, ESPRIT), beamforming for LoS and mmWave settings;

 Basics of OFDM – Transmitter and receiver block diagrams, cyclic prefix, subcarrier orthogonality, FFT/IFFT operations.

 MIMO-OFDM TX/RX signal processing – Resource mapping, pilot structure, synchronization signals, channel estimation (Schmidl-Cox, LS/MMSE), frequency-domain equalization (ZF/MMSE), iterative equalization, precoding, demodulation;

 OFDM impairments and Mitigation Techniques – Peak-to-Average Power Ratio (PAPR), Inter-Carrier Interference (ICI), Carrier Frequency Offset (CFO), timing mismatch, spectral leakage.

 Applications and Emerging Topics – MIMO-OFDM link-level simulation, Massive MIMO, mmWave communication, Integrated Sensing and Communications (ISAC).

EE 672

#### Field Practices in Earth System Sciences

#### Credit L | T | P | C 0 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Course Content

# Field Practices in Earth System Sciences [EH 301]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
0 | 0 | 0 | 2

Goals/ tasks

 1. Field geology (rock, feature, structure identification on different spatial and temporal scales): Training for developing skills as an independent field geologist. Focus is to learn what to look for in the field and how to identify and scientifically explain the feature you observed.
 2. Learning a few easy mobile devices (compass, brunton, clinometer) and topo sheet etc.
 3. Mapping and /or sample collection techniques: Rock/ sand/soil/water sample collection for various analytical purposes. Collection of oriented rock samples and learning how to procure samples for various geochronological measurements.
 4. Reading/analyzing features and documentation.
 5. Report writing: A detailed field report has to be submitted within 10 days of completion of the fieldwork and students will be evaluated on the basis of the report and performance in the field.

Skills that can be achieved

 1. Identifying geological and geomorphic features, sample collection and process interpretation.
 2. Bridging field data with model simulations.
 3. Marine Structures, Life and Pollution.
 4. Features of the biodiversity/national forest: Components, Conservation and Survey

Prospect areas

 1. Mountain belt/ hills: Understanding the geology of the hills and rock formations, finding structural features showing active tectonics and surface features, erosion etc. These areas need extensive geological mapping. It will be a mapping intensive course. (Kutch-Bhuj area, Aravalli range near Gujarat-Rajasthan border, Deccan traps in northern Maharastra, Northwestern Himalaya etc.)
 2. Beach / coastal areas: Study of sedimentary features and the role of sea. Understanding the vegetation. Study of nearby hills and their geology. Their role in sediment transport and above all, how sea level change occurs and how does it affect the landscape and surface processes (e.g.: Cambay area, Karnataka coast, south-Maharastra coast etc.)
 3. National parks and Sanctuaries: Geology, vegetation and fauna of the national park areas. Understanding the behaviour of the fauna. Mapping will not be possible in the national parks, but in nearby areas. (e.g. Gir National park, Ranthambore national park, Bandhavgarh National Park, Pench Tiger reserve etc.).
 4. River systems and water bodies: Understanding the river systems and their evolution. Role of tides in river confluences near sea and related deposits. How vegetation and fauna depend on the dynamic nature of the delta. (e.g.: Narmada delta).
 5. Mining Areas: Mine visits are essential for understanding how geology and engineering gets coupled. The focus is to learn the geology and genesis of the ore mineral, understanding the mining techniques, safety protocols and reserve estimation. (e.g.: Coal mines in central India, manganese mines in Maharashtra, lignite mines in Gujarat).
 6. Landslides and landfill: Understanding the processes related to landslides, different type of landslides, methodology for slope stability analysis.

For each year one/two prospect areas will be selected by the instructors.

 L | T | P | C

Stream Management: Fluvial hazards and their causes, Humans and rivers, Ecosystem based approach to stream management, Concept of river health, Environmental Flow (e-flow) – definition, data requirement, different approaches for e-flow estimation.

EH 609

#### Engineering Seismology and Seismic Hazard Analysis

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Engineering Seismology and Seismic Hazard Analysis [EH 610]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction of earthquake hazard, historical perspective for effects of earthquakes, tsunamis, liquefaction and landslides, major earthquakes in India and in the world, overview of seismic zonation and codes, national and global seismic hazard mapping programs. Overview of plate tectonics, types of faults, earthquake source, source models. Quantification of earthquake size: earthquake energy, magnitude scales, magnitude relations, intensity scale. Wave propagation and attenuation: body waves, surface waves, geometrical spreading and inelastic attenuation, scattering. Earthquake recordings and ground motion parameters: displacement, velocity and acceleration motions, response spectra, spectral acceleration, Fourier spectra. Earthquake location and magnitude determination using seismic records. Brief overview of probability theory: Basic principles, subjective probability, conditional probability theorem, random variables and distributions. Seismic hazard analysis methods: deterministic and probabilistic methods, types of sources and source zonation, earthquake catalogues and seismicity analysis, ground motion models, ground motion simulations, hazard integral and hazard computation, types of uncertainties and their treatment in typical seismic hazard studies, UHS (Uniform Hazard Spectrum). Examples of Seismic hazard studies, e.g., GSHAP (Global Seismic Hazard Assessment Program), and other international programs. Overview of site-specific seismic hazard analysis and micro-zonation, local site-effects, site response analysis methods and site-classifications. State of the practice of seismic hazard analysis post Fukushima incident and examples of some site-specific seismic hazard studies for NPPs (Nuclear Power Plants) around the world. Overview of seismic risk and vulnerability, difference between hazard and risk.

#### Near Surface Geophysics

 Course Content

# Near Surface Geophysics [EH 611]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

-  Physical fields of the Earth: Gravitational field, Geomagnetic field; electromagnetic field, seismic wave-field, thermal field.
-  Properties of the Earth and processes: Mechanical properties, density, elasticity, seismic waves; Electrical properties (conductivity and resistivity) of the rocks; Magnetic properties of rocks, soils, Remanent magnetization; Gravity anomalies, Earth’s heat, temperature inside the Earth; Heat transport in the Earth.
-  Measurements of physical fields and rock properties: Gravimeter and gravity measurements, Satellite based gravity measurements, working principle of magnetometer, seismometer and GPR (Ground Penetrating Radar).
-  Methods of geophysical interpretation: Direct and inverse methods, gravity, magnetic electrical surveying and electromagnetic surveying; Seismic reflection and refraction data processing and interpretation; GPR data processing and interpretation.
-  Applications: Applications of geophysical methods in the field of groundwater, geotechnical, civil infrastructure, mining, hydrocarbon exploration using existing data.

 Sea-level fluctuations and Coastlines, Coral reefs and Ocean Acidification; Plastic Pollution; Future Predictions on Ocean and Global Change

 L | T | P | C

 Earthquake Physics

 Distribution of Earthquake in and around the Indian plate, Objective estimation of magnitude of completeness, Declustering long term models of earthquakes for Indian earthquakes, Making a long-term probabilistic model for Indian earthquakes

 Course Content

# Materials of Earth’s Deep Interior [EH 616]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Introduction to the Earth’s interior:
 Origin of the Solar System: Types of planets, Proto-Sun, planetesimals.
 • Chemical and isotopic composition of meteorites: Types of Meteorites, difference and similarities with Earth composition.
 • Planetary models
 • Hadean Earth: Magma Ocean, Core formation, Atmosphere formation.
 • Internal structure of the Earth and its significance: Overview to Layers of the Earth.
 • Major Seismic discontinuities: Gutenberg, Moho etc.
Crystal Structure:
 • Crystal Systems and Space Groups: Introduction to Space groups and fundamental crystallography.
 • Symmetry: Crystal symmetry with applications to deep earth.
 • Major minerals in the earth’s interior: Olivines, Pyroxenes, Spinel, Perovskite etc.
 • Phase transitions.
 • Phase diagrams.
 • Experimental techniques in mineral physics: X-ray diffraction and Spectroscopy.
 • Introduction to synchrotron sources
 • Theoretical techniques and their applications: Density functional theory and its applications to Earth sciences.
 • Equations of State: Birch Murnaghan, Vinet, Mie-Grüneisen.
 • Thermodynamics and its applications in geophysics: Laws of thermodynamics, Grüneisen parameter and its importance.
Mantle:
 • Composition of the upper and lower mantle: Composition of the Early Mantle and its evolution.
 • Heat Flow: Fundamentals of heat-transfer.
 • Geochemical signatures of the mantle.
 • Seismological signatures of the mantle: Heterogeneities, Anisotropy of Mantle.
 • Elastic constants, Hooke’s Law: Fundamentals of Elasticity; Elastic tensor.
 • Elastic moduli and their applications: Calculation and experimental techniques for determination of elastic constants.
 • Elastic anisotropy
 • Pressure and temperature profile of the mantle: Geothermal gradient, Adiabats, Isentropes.
 • Constraints and uncertainties on the mantle temperatures.
 • Rheology: Determination of viscosity; rheological Models.

EH 619

#### Sequence Stratigraphy

#### Credit L | T | P | C 1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Course Content

# Sequence Stratigraphy [EH 620]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 History of sequence stratigraphy, basic terminology, definitions and sequence stratigraphic method applied to cores and well logs. Vertical stacking, surfaces and systems tracts, the accommodation succession method, and the sequence stratigraphy hierarchy. Fundamentals of seismic stratigraphy, seismic response of stratigraphic surfaces, seismic reflection termination mapping and seismic facies analyses. Wheeler diagram, hydrocarbon play element recognition and mapping, reservoir architecture, interpretation and mapping techniques from exploration to production business scales. Practicals: 1) Lithostratigraphy vs chronostratigraphy, 2) Beds, bedsets, and parasequences, 3) Incised Valleys in outcrop and cores, 4) Well-logs to cores, 5) Idealized depositional sequence, 6) Tank experiment, 7) Seismic forward models and idealized depositional sequence, 8) Terminations interpretation in seismic, 9) Sequence, sequence sets, and composite sequence, 10) Well-log seismic tie, 11) Chronostratigraphy cycle charts, 12) Wheeler diagram

# Climate of the Past [EH 621]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C

Climate and Climatic Variation, Introduction to Paleoclimate Reconstruction, Base Maps for Paleoclimatology, Refining Paleolatitudes, Proxies: Elemental and Isotopic Proxies (δ18O for changes in salinity, δ13C for carbon cycle, Mg/Ca for paleotemperature, Boron isotopes for paleo-CO2 reconstruction respectively), Biological proxy datasets would be used.

EH 621

#### Fluid Flow and Reactions in Reservoir Rocks

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Fluid Flow and Reactions in Reservoir Rocks [EH 622]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction to rock properties governing fluid flow: porosity, permeability, permeability tensor, capillary pressure, relative permeability, Darcy’s law for single- and two-phase systems, drainage and imbibition, saturation functions, pressure build-up.
 Groundwater flow: Vadose and phreatic zones, groundwater table, aquifer-aquitard, fluid potential, groundwater flow direction, hydrostatic equilibrium, conductivity-storativity-transmissivity, impact of heterogeneity and anisotropy, Darcy’s law for groundwater systems, transient groundwater equation, tracer tests, diffusion and dispersion, 2 phase groundwater contamination.
 Groundwater geochemistry: Major and minor groundwater species, TDS-conductivity-alkalinity-redox potential, activity and fugacity, activity variation with concentration, phase solubility, delta-delta plots, Stiff and Piper plots, reactive contaminants, contaminant retardation, acidic contaminants and reaction with rocks, contamination remediation.
 Geological CO2 storage: CO2 density variation with depth, gas migration under different force balances, viscous-capillary-gravity equilibrium, structural-capillary-solubility-mineral trapping, lithological heterogeneity, Leverett-J scaling, snap-off and capillary heterogeneity mechanisms, impact of salinity on solubility, mineral dissolution and precipitation, impact of flow rate on reaction kinetics.

 Case study: Paleoclimate and Sedimentation in the Indo-Gangetic basins
 Case study: BioStratigraphy of Petroliferous Basins of Bombay offshore, Kutch, Kerala Konkan regions, focus on paleoclimate studies of Paleogene and Neogene in Indian subcontinent
 Case study: Permian Triassic (Guruyur Ravine, Spiti) K/Pg (Deccan, Meghalaya)

#### Ceramics in Archaeology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Ceramics in Archaeology [EH 629]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Overview of ceramics, their origin, types (pottery, stoneware, faience), and artistic significance (figurines). Includes the earliest examples globally and in India. Theoretical framework on the importance of studying ceramics; Covers typology, seriation, classification, and documentation. Explores ceramic production processes, how pots were made, open-pit and updraft kilns. Discusses decoration types and cultural significance, scientific approaches to ceramic analysis including provenance, petrology, microstructural, macrostructural and manufacturing technology. Various production methods and scientific investigations. Comparable faience technology in Egyptian and Mesopotamian civilizations. Experimental research conducted by scholars and different methods of analysis; Examination of figurines within archaeological contexts, focusing on their cultural and social importance; technology of production. Analysis of bricks, including their production processes, architectural applications, and evolution over time; Ethnographic surveys to the potter’s workshop to understand the manufacturing processes of pottery; Hands-on handling and better understanding of pottery from various cultural stages, spanning from the Harappan period to the Historical period.

#### Statistics for Earth Sciences

 Course Content

# Statistics for Earth Sciences [EH 639]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Foundations of Statistical Thinking in Earth Sciences: Discrete and continuous data, Descriptive statistics: Central Tendency (mean, median, mode) and Dispersion (variance, standard deviation). Model Validation: Bias–variance decomposition, bootstrap methods, and cross-validation. Spatial and Temporal Data Basics time series analysis (autocorrelation). Hands-on projects with Earth science datasets.

ES 106B

#### Computing

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 3 | 4

 Course Content

# Computing [ES 112]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 3 | 4

1. Elementary Programming Techniques

- Building familiarity with instruction driven task completion using Scratch or CS Unplugged.
- Von Neumann architecture, the translation process for a program (Source code -> Interpreter/Compiler -> Machine code)
- Expressions: values or literals, combining values using operators, basic types (int, float, str, bool); type conversion; lazy evaluation for Boolean operators.
- Functions (scope), examples from math and random; writing your own simple functions that do not require control flow; (e.g, “celsius_to_fahrenheit”)
- Program development lifecycle (edit, test, debug; libraries such as pytest, simple_benchmark; and tools such as flake8)
- Control flow: conditionals (if-else, match); loops (while, for).
2. Advanced Data Types

- Tuples: construction, unpacking.
- Lists: construction (+ comprehensions), indexing, slicing.
- Sets: construction, selection, set operations.
- Dictionaries: construction, indexing, update, merging.
- Complex data (dictionaries of lists to lists etc.); frozenset, frozendict.
- Objects and Classes
- Exceptions: try-catch-finally, writing your own exceptions.
3. Advanced Programming Techniques

- Recursion (basic examples such as Fibonacci and Towers of Hanoi)
- Functions as values (functools)
- Iterators and generators (itertools, more-itertools)
4. Programming in the Large

- Modules: code organization
- Selection of python modules (e.g, re, numpy, scipy, beautifulsoup, flask, etc).

ES 112

#### Data-Centric Computing

#### Credit L | T | P | C 1.5 | 0 | 1.5 | 3

 Course Content

# Data-Centric Computing [ES 113]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1.5 | 0 | 1.5 | 3

Algorithms for processing data: Understanding time complexity – Asymptotics (big-O notation and examples); Storing sequential data: arrays and linked lists, data structures for storing tabular data; Organizing data and finding items – Searching (linear/binary search), Sorting – sorting almost sorted data (bubble sort, insertion sort), fast sorting in the worst-case (heapsort), using randomization to sort faster (quicksort), sorting without random-access ex. linked-lists (mergesort); Data Analysis: Tools - Spreadsheets (Excel/Google Sheet/Calc); data manipulation specific Python libraries e.g. Pandas; introduction to SQL; Answering queries over tabular data: map-filter-reduce, joins and unions. Using vectorized operations for efficiency. Techniques: Data structuring (tidy data) and cleaning (dealing with multiple labels for the same data, missing data) Summarizing data (averages, variance, moments), functions of tables (loading, cleaning, normalizing); Visualizing data introduction to matplotlib; plotting various statistics; categorical distributions, numerical distributions, overlaid graphs; Deriving Conclusions: Anomaly detection techniques in data: tests e.g. GRIM test, Benford’s law; Statistical traps: Correlation vs causation, Base-rate fallacy / prosecutor's fallacy. Simpson's paradox. Data censoring; Will Rogers effect, lead-time bias, and length time bias. Means versus medians. Importance of higher moments. Misleading visualizations; Making Predictions: Linear regression; basics of classification (train, test, validation); creating features; naive Bayes classification, logistic regression and its interpretation; introduction to clustering (k-means). Confidence in predictions: prediction intervals, confidence intervals.

3 | 1 | 1.5 | 5

Introduction:

relationship between electrical engineering and the physical world; Signals and Sources: concept of a signal; physical origin of signals, types of signals (temporal, spatial, slow, rapid, periodic, aperiodic); independent and dependent sources; voltage sources, current sources, impedance matching; Measures of a signal: amplitude, peak value, average value, rms value, repetition rate, duty cycle; phasor representation; Spectral analysis of signals: elementary concepts of the Fourier series and the Fourier transform Types of systems: linear and nonlinear systems; active and passive systems; Signal gathering: basic properties of sensors, linearity, dynamic range, time response, cross-sensitivity Energy storage: the role of reactive circuit elements; effects of capacitance and inductance, time-domain properties of systems, transient behaviour of system described by first-order differential equations; Frequency selectivity: frequency response of systems, low-pass, high-pass and resonant behaviour described by second-order differential equations; Signal conditioning: concept of amplification, operational amplifiers as amplifiers; Signal conditioning: filtering, use of operational amplifiers with reactive circuit elements Analog-to-digital conversion: sampling and quantization; Noise: elementary ideas about types and sources of noise, signal-to-noise ratio; Nonlinear circuit elements: the diode and simple diode circuits, the MOSFET as an amplifier and as a switch Combinational Logic: brief of logic gates, minimization of logic expressions; Sequential Logic: Latch, Flip-flops (SR, JK, D), flip-flop applications (frequency counter, registers, decoders, multiplexers, demultiplexers, comparators); Overview of applications of EE in important areas: renewable energy, electric vehicles, communication and signal processing.

ES 204

#### Thermodynamics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 2 | 1 | 0 | 3

 Course Content

# Thermodynamics [ES 211]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
2 | 1 | 0 | 3

 Introduction and basic concepts
 Thermodynamics & energy; applications of thermodynamics; importance of dimensions and units; systems and control volumes; properties of a system; state and equilibrium; processes and cycles; temperature and the zeroth of thermodynamics; generic problem-solving technique in thermodynamics

 Energy, Energy transfer and general energy analysis
 Forms of energy; energy transfer due to heat, work and matter; mechanical forms of work; the first law of thermodynamics; energy conversion efficiencies; energy and the environment

 Properties of pure substances
 Phases of a pure substance; phase-change processes; property diagrams and tables; link between classical and statistical properties; equations of state

 Energy analysis of closed systems
 Moving boundary work; energy balance for closed systems; specific heats; internal energy, enthalpy and specific heats for ideal gases

 Mass and energy analysis of control volumes
 Conservation of mass; flow work and the energy of a flowing fluid; energy analysis of flow systems; steady flow systems; unsteady flow processes

 The second law of thermodynamics
 Thermal energy reservoirs; heat engines, refrigerators and heat pumps; reversible and irreversible processes; internally reversible processes; Carnot cycle and principles; Carnot heat engine and refrigerator

 Entropy
 Clausius inequality; increase of entropy principle; entropy change of pure substances; property diagrams; isentropic processes; reversible steady-flow work; isentropic efficiency for steady flow devices; entropy balance

 Introduction to thermodynamic cycles
 Introduction to Rankine, Diesel and Brayton cycles for power production, mobility and aviation; introduction to vapor compression refrigeration cycle

 Exergy
 Work potential of energy; reversible work and irreversibility; second-law efficiency; exergy change of a system; exergy balance for closed systems and control volumes

 Thermodynamics of radiation
 Planck’s law for black bodies; Wien’s displacement law; absorption and emission of radiation; black bodies; Stefan-Boltzmann law

 Fundamentals of photovoltaic energy conversion
 Properties of sunlight; solar cell operation

 L | T | P | C

 1. Basics of optimization: variables, constraints, objectives, linear, nonlinear, mixed-integer, convex, nonconvex, formulation and solution using computer solver
 2. Basics of process control: proportional, proportional-integral, and proportional-integral-derivative controllers, dynamic & multi-variable process modeling using computer software, and closed-loop simulation under different controllers
 3. Data analytics: outlier removal, dimensionality reduction, principle component analysis, feature selection, k-means clustering, hierarchical clustering, support vector machines, random forest classification
 4. Data-driven modeling and optimization: design of experiments, sampling, response surface model, neural network model, validation, surrogate-based optimization
 5. Model predictive control (MPC): introduction to MPC, data-driven state-space model development, MPC controller development using state-space model, closed loop simulation under MPC.
 The course will use datasets familiar to engineers, such as industrial process data, energy & environmental systems data, and semiconductor manufacturing data.

# Integrated Circuit Fabrication Lab [ES 339]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C

# Networks and Complex Systems [ES 404]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

ES 408

#### Introduction to Inverse Modelling in Physical Sciences

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Introduction to Inverse Modelling in Physical Sciences [ES 410]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Review: Mathematical prerequisites; Nature of mathematical modeling in physical sciences; Classification of inverse problems: discrete (parameter estimation) and continuous inverse problems. Discretizing continuous inverse problems using basis functions. ; Nature of ill-posed problems. Examples of ill-posed problems in physical sciences ; Inverse solutions using least squares methods. Rank deficiency and ill-conditioning: Singular Value Decomposition and generalized inverse; covariance and resolution of the generalized inverse solution. Minimum norm inverse solutions. model resolution and information density matrices. ; Tikhonov regularization: resolution, bias and uncertainty in solution, discrepancy method. Higher order Tikhonov regularization-Other regularization techniques: Sparsity regularization, bounds constraints; Fourier techniques: regularization in Fourier space. ; Stochastic inverse theory, Bayesian inverse theory: multivariate normal case, Markov Chain Monte Carlo methods, Analyzing MCMC outputs. Using stochastic methods for identifying a host of acceptable models. ; Nonlinear inverse modeling: Occam's inversion.

# Deep Learning [ES 413]

 L | T | P | C

Recent Advances.

# Introduction to Photonics [ES 414]

 L | T | P | C

ES 414

#### Nature-inspired Materials Design

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Nature-inspired Materials Design [ES 415]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

I. Preliminaries of biomimetic materials
 - Principles of biomimicry from the fields of biology, engineering and design
 - Processing and characterization of Biomimetic materials
 - Models and simulations of structure-property relationships in biomimetics

II. Strong, tough and lightweight materials
 - Strengthening and toughening mechanisms inspired by spider silk, nacre and hard tissues
 - Applications in design of nature-inspired composites with superior mechanical properties

III. Wear and impact-resistant materials
 - Structure and design inspiration from hard and wear resistant materials such as teeth, bones, and horns
 - Nano-scale mechanisms of wear and impact resistance

IV. Self-shaping and self-healing materials
 - Reversible/switchable properties such as stiffness, strength and shape inspired by sea cucumber and squid beak
 - Self-healing properties from plants and tree-bark inspiring vascular networks
 - Nanocomposites and concretes demonstrating self-shaping and self-healing properties

V. Self-cleaning materials and surfaces
 - Fundamentals of wettability and self-cleaning
 - Superhydrophobicity (Lotus effect) and self-cleaning dry adhesives inspired by Gecko feet.

VI. Materials for optical applications
 - Diffraction gratings, reflectors and photonic materials inspired by nature
 - Nature-inspired antireflective surfaces and lenses

VII. Special manufacturing methods for advanced biomimetic materials

ES 653

#### Medical Imaging Systems

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Medical Imaging Systems [ES 655]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 The goal of this course is to provide an overview of standard clinical imaging modalities from the signals and systems perspective. Ultrasound, X-ray, Computer Tomography (CT), and Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) will be covered. The course will address image acquisition and reconstruction for respective imaging modalities, choice of the appropriate modality for specific applications, modality- specific issues on image quality, clinical applications, and safety- related considerations for each approach. The students will be introduced to metrics for assessing imaging performance and diagnostic value. Examples from classic and current literature will be discussed. The following topics will be covered: 1. Review of linear systems, 2-dimensional Fourier Transform, electromagnetic spectrum, acoustic spectrum, attenuation, scattering 2. Ultrasound imaging: Acoustic propagation, reflection, diffraction, attenuation, Doppler effect, tissue properties, speckle, transducer probes, devices, image artifacts, safety 3. Conventional X-ray: X-ray sources, tissue properties, artifacts, noise/resolution 4. Computed tomography: Projection, central-slice theorem, image reconstruction, backprojection, filtering, convolution, noise, artifacts, safety 5. 5. Magnetic Resonance Imaging: Larmor frequency, tissue properties, rotating frame, gradients, excitation pulses, Fourier transform relations, devices, artifacts, and safety

 L | T | P | C

The proposed course will provide the conceptual foundations and practical insights for the use of ultrasound in imaging and therapy, encompassing physics, instrumentation, signal generation/processing, and clinical applications.

ES 659

#### Variational Methods in Mechanics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Variational Methods in Mechanics [ES 660]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Mathematical preliminaries: Basics of multivariable differential calculus, maxima and minima for a general function of n variables, constraints and Lagrange multipliers, examples; Mathematical preliminaries: Deﬁnition of a function, metrics and metric space, norm and vector spaces, function spaces relevant to engineering and applied sciences, definition of a ‘functional’ and its properties; Historical genesis of calculus of variations, classical problems beginning with the works of Fermat, Bernoulli, Euler, Lagrange, Jacobi, Weierstrass, Hamilton, Legendre and others, examples from the domain of applied mechanics, dynamics, thermal sciences, control theory, design optimization; Definition and physical significance of variational derivative, variation of a functional. Necessary and sufficiency condition for extremum or stationarity of a functional, the Euler’s equation, invariance of Euler’s equation. Canonical form and first integrals of the Euler equations, Legendre and canonical transformations, Noether's theorem, principle of least action, conservation laws; Variational methods in statics (principles of minimum potential energy and virtual work), extended-Hamilton’s principle, Hamilton-Jacobi differential equation, examples of variational method and its application in continuum mechanics, elastodynamics, evolution equations and boundary conditions for one- and two-dimensional structural members (bars, beams, membranes); Variational problems with fixed and movable boundaries, broken extremals, Weierstrass-Erdmann condition and general jump conditions (examples from continuum mechanics); Deﬁnition of the second variation and the strong/weak minimizer, necessary and suﬃcient condition for a minimum, Legendre and Jacobi conditions, Weierstrass E-function, field of extremals; Minimum characterization of eigenvalue problems, Sturm-Liouville problems, Rayleigh quotient for vibration of continuum, buckling and stability analysis of elastic columns; Direct methods of solving variational problems, Ritz method and the method of finite differences

ES 660

#### Probabilistic Machine Learning

 Course Content

# Probabilistic Machine Learning [ES 661]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Probability refresher: Probability theory, discrete distributions, continuous distributions, joint probability distributions, sampling from different distributions (e.g. using Box-Muller transform), uncertainty modelling, information theoretic concepts: (KL-divergence, entropy); Bayesian concept learning: Likelihood, Prior, Posterior, Maximum Likelihood Estimate (MLE), Maximum A-Posteriori estimation (MAP), Full Bayesian Estimation with Conjugate Priors (Beta-Bernoulli model for the coin toss example, Normal-Normal model for estimating parameters of Normal distribution); Bayesian supervised methods: Regression: Bayesian linear regression, Robust linear regression via alternative likelihood (e.g. Laplace, Student-T); Classification: Bayesian Logistic regression, Bayesian Naive Bayes; Latent Variable modelling: Gaussian Mixture Model, Probabilistic principal component analysis (PPCA); Approximate Inference: Sampling based strategies: Rejection sampling, Importance sampling, Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC), Metropolis Hastings, Gibbs sampling, No U-Turn sampler (NUTS); Variational inference: Mean field approach, Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO), Reparameterization trick, Stochastic Variational Inference, Automatic Differentiation Variational Inference (ADVI); Gaussian Processes (GP): Multivariate Normal distribution and its properties, Kernels, GP regression, GP classification, Approximate Inference and Sparse GPs; Bayesian Optimization (BO) and Active Learning (AL): AL - Query by committee, Uncertainty sampling, Expected model change, BO - Acquisition functions, GP based BO, Random Forests based BO; Probabilistic Deep Learning: MC Dropout, Deep Ensembles, Bayesian neural networks (BNNs), Deep GPs.

Diffusion Models and their Applications. Variational Autoencoders. Attention Mechanism and Transformers with Applications. Normalising Flows. Recent Advances.

 Advanced Deep Learning Tools and Techniques: Design and Implementation - 3D CNN, Transfer Learning, Domain Adaptation, Neural Tangent Kernel. Deep Learning on Graphs - Embeddings, Graph Neural Networks, and their Applications. Self-Supervised Learning. Uncertainty Quantification in Deep Learning. Tips and Tricks to enhance the Performance of Deep Neural Network implementations. Recent Advances.

 Applications: Research domains - Sustainability, Computer Vision, Natural Language Processing, Web Mining, Signal Processing, Bioinformatics, Climate Modeling, and Others.

ES 667

#### 5G and Beyond

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# 5G and Beyond [ES 668]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Overview of 5G: Evolution, standardization, application potential in IoT, AR/VR, Smart infra and Autonomous Vehicles. Core, RAN and air interface, protocol stack and layers. 5G physical layer, air interface and RAN: Millimeter wave propagation characteristics, Modulation and demodulation. multiplexing and multiple access. OFDM, OFDMA. Multiple antenna techniques, Air interface protocol stack, frequency and time domain structures, numerologies. radio interface and access protocol.
 5G virtualization: Software defined networks, Architecture and Principles: Control and Data Planes, separation of control and data, SDN use cases. ES 668

#### Advanced Computer Architecture and Systems

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Advanced Computer Architecture and Systems [ES 669]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction to Computer Architecture and Systems: Overview of computer architecture, historical context and evolution. Performance metrics and measurements, benchmarking techniques, Amdhal’s law, Gustafson-Barsis law, Little’s law and their implication. Instruction set Architecture, MIPS/RISC-V and CISC.
 Parallel Architecture: Instruction Level Parallelism: pipelined processor design, superscalar and out-of-order execution techniques. Dynamic scheduling, register renaming, scoreboarding, Tumasulo’s algorithm, speculative execution. Multi-core and many core systems, Data level and thread level parallelism.
 Memory Hierarchy: Cache and Memory hierarchy, advanced techniques for cache optimization. Distributed Shared memory, Coherence protocols (MSI, MESI, MOSI). Memory Consistency models, Sequential Consistency and relaxed consistency models. Advanced cache and memory bandwidth control and monitoring using allocation technologies, RDT.
 GPU Architecture and Programming: Massively parallel processors, SIMT model, synchronous and asynchronous memory updates, unified virtual addressing and unified memory. Fundamentals of CUDA programming.
 Advanced topics on Heterogeneous and Emerging Architecture: Interconnection networks, Network-on-chip, Accelerators: near, in-memory and neuromorphic computing, NPU, DPU and TPUs. Approximate computing, intermittent and energy-harvesting computation.

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 L | T | P | C

Applications (from): Audio Signal Processing, Image Processing, Computer Vision, Biomedical Engineering, Computer Graphics, Optics, Machine Learning, Deep Learning, and Data Science.

ES 670

#### Mechanics of Composite Materials

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Mechanics of Composite Materials [ES 671]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

1. Review of solid mechanics, foundations of tensor analysis

 2. Introduction to composites: classification and advantages

 3. Micro-mechanics of a lamina: Strength of materials approach and brief introduction to elasticity approach

 4. Macro-mechanics of a lamina
 • 3-D constitutive laws for anisotropic, monoclinic, orthotropic, transversely isotropic and isotropic bodies
 • 2-D constitutive law for specially orthotropic and generally orthotropic layers made up of unidirectional fiber composite
 • Experimental measurements of lamina / laminate material constants
 • Failure theories of an angle lamina
 • Hygrothermal stresses and strains in a lamina

 5. Macro-mechanics of a laminate
 • Classical laminate theory
 • Laminate constitutive law for various layups
 • Hygrothermal stresses and strains in a laminate
 • Stretching problems of symmetric laminates
 • Bending problems: Navier-type solutions for symmetric and unsymmetric cross-ply layups and for antisymmetric angle-ply layups.
 • Brief introduction to free vibration and buckling analysis: Navier-type solutions

 6. Comparison between different failure theories, progressive failure analysis of a composite laminate

 7. Interlaminar stresses and delamination

 8. Brief introduction to shear deformation theories

 Course Content

# Semiconductor Packaging [ES 672]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

Module 1: Introduction to Semiconductor Packaging (8 hours)
 • Brief overview of ICs highlighting FEOL and BEOL to pad
 • Scaling guidelines for Front-end (Moore’s Law) and Back-end
 • Anatomy and function of semiconductor packaging
 • Various types of packages (difference in choice of materials and designs)
 • Surface mount and through hole packages

Module 2: Semiconductor Packaging Manufacturing and Testing (12 hours)
 • Description of semiconductor assembly and test factory
 • Learn about the various stages of semiconductor package manufacturing
 • Testing of Electronic Packages (Setup and Operation of Test Equipment, Wafer Level & Component Level Testing Process Flows)

Module 3: Fundamentals of Package design (8 hours)
 • Package design for required electrical, thermal and mechanical performance
 • Design flows for solving the electrical, thermal and mechanical challenges of a PCB

Module 4: Reliability and Quality (6 hours)
 • Reliability testing & Qualification – MST/MSL, TC/TS, HAST, HTSL, Lead Integrity, Solderability, Board level test
 • Role of Process Control Systems in semiconductor manufacturing
 • Using control charts to monitor process performance

Module 5: Advanced Semiconductor Packaging (6 hours)
 • Introduction to Pathway for Assembly and Packaging technologies
 • The evolution and impact of packaging on product performance and innovation.

ES 673

#### Process Data Analytics, Optimization, and Control

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Process Data Analytics, Optimization, and Control [ES 674]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 1. Basics of optimization: variables, constraints, objectives, linear, nonlinear, mixed-integer, convex, nonconvex, formulation and solution using computer solver
 2. Basics of process control: proportional, proportional-integral, and proportional-integral-derivative controllers, dynamic & multi-variable process modeling using computer software, and closed-loop simulation under different controllers
 3. Data analytics: outlier removal, dimensionality reduction, principle component analysis, feature selection, k-means clustering, hierarchical clustering, support vector machines, random forest classification
