 5. Model predictive control (MPC): introduction to MPC, data-driven state-space model development, MPC controller development using state-space model, closed loop simulation under MPC.
 The course will use datasets familiar to engineers, such as industrial process data, energy & environmental systems data, and semiconductor manufacturing data.

 Course Content

# Photonics – Principles and Applications [ES 675]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Basic properties of light and classical treatment of light-matter interaction; origin of refractive index, absorption, dispersion; basic optical phenomena - interference, diffraction, birefringence; Generation of light: basic operation of light-emitting diodes and lasers; Lab demonstrations of important temporal and spectral properties of these sources; Controlling the properties of light to convey information; modulation of intensity, phase and polarization; electro-optic modulators, acousto-optic modulators, polarizers; splitters, combiners, multiplexers; Propagation of light through dielectric waveguide structures – conventional and unconventional optical fiber; rectangular waveguide structures; metal-dielectric waveguides; Detection of light: p-i-n photodiodes, avalanche photodiodes; quantum efficiency, responsivity, spectral and temporal response; Applications in engineering and science such as optical fiber communication, optical sensing of physical quantities (displacement, speed, strain, vibration, temperature) and chemicals (high-resolution molecular spectroscopy to identify different gases), plasmonics, biophotonics, imaging, light detection and ranging (LIDAR) as used in distributed sensing and autonomous cars. Lab demonstrations of sensing displacement, vibration, temperature and gas leaks.

#### Classical Information Theory

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Classical Information Theory [ES 676]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 1. Review of Real Analysis (rigorous and abstract) and probability theory;
 2. Information measures: entropy (joint, relative and condition);
 3. Asymptotic Equipartition Property (AEP) and typicality;

 5. Reliable communication and channel capacity – One shot and Asymptotic;
 6. Information measures for continuous random variables;
 7. AWGN channel and its capacity;
 8. Lossy compression and rate distortion – One shot and Asymptotic;
 9. Topics in Network Information Theory (MAC, Broadcast Channel Coding and Slepian Wolf Theorem);
 10. Basic Hypothesis Testing and Error Exponents (Time Permitting).

 L | T | P | C

ES 678

#### RFIC Design

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 3 | 5

 Course Content

# RFIC Design [ES 679]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 3 | 5

 Introduction to RF and Transmission Line Theory covering signal behavior, reflection coefficients, VSWR, and impedance matching using the Smith Chart for lumped and distributed elements; RF Network Representation focusing on S-parameters for circuit characterization and shifting of reference planes; Wireless Architectures and CMOS Integration exploring transceiver blocks, IC design constraints, and the transition from discrete components to Silicon microelectronics; High-Frequency Transistor Modeling and Low Noise Amplifiers (LNA) focusing on f, T, fmax, and parasitic effects to implement inductively degenerated CMOS topologies with optimized noise figures; Mixer Design and Frequency Translation encompassing active Gilbert Cells and passive CMOS switching mixers while addressing conversion gain and port isolation; Oscillators and Frequency Synthesis covering negative resistance theory, LC and Ring Oscillator topologies, phase noise, and the basics of Phase Locked Loops (PLL); Amplifier and CMOS Power Amplifier (PA) Design focusing on stability, Class A-C operations, and efficiency (PAE) for large-signal performance; System Integration addressing transceiver challenges such as IQ imbalance, DC offsets, and the role of integrated filters in signal chains.

# Cultures of Communication [FP 601]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
2 | 0 | 2 | 4

 This course provides an induction for a graduate student to enter into fields of thought, action and communication with other researchers from divergent disciplines as well as ideologies. Structured like a foundation, the course will focus on specific themes each year to be handled by a group of faculty members. Specific themes may include: listening ‘silences’; decoding the visual; the uncertainty principle; peer group evaluation; conversation across disciplines; philosophical assumptions and knowledge making; activism as communication; How to ‘read’ arguments in quantitative and qualitative research. The course will include activities such as the following: Panel discussions, field visits, invited speakers, group discussions, self-analysis through diagnostic tests; cross-disciplinary perspectives through observations studies.

FP 602

#### General Education 1 “Experiencing Hindustani Raga Music”

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Course Content

# General Education 1 “Experiencing Hindustani Raga Music” [GE 101]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Survey of various fundamental aspects of Hindustani raga music including: (1) a general overview of classical Indian music history; (2) a delineation of basic theoretical concepts relating to raga and tala including arohi, avarohi, raga taxonomy, thāt theory, melakarta system, etc.; (3) a review of different raga music genres (dhrupad, khayal, thumri) along with a survey of influential artists of the twentieth century; and (4) an exploration of Indian aesthetics (rasa theory) and its relation to the idea of transcendence through art.

GE 101

#### English Studies

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# English Studies [HS 101]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This course is designed to provide students with the necessary tools to improve their practical use of communicative English. To this end, the course will include the following four patterns of learning. Listening: accurate, complete and coherent grasp of basic content in lectures as well as dialogues/conversations; Speaking: development of confidence in the use of English language for basic spoken expression at the interpersonal level; Reading: culturally familiar semi-technical and non-technical readings for the purposes of basic comprehension of content, as well as advanced comprehension of underlying compositional structures, development of ideas, and modes of assimilation (understanding, retention, analytical/critical engagement); Writing: vocabulary (scope and correctness/appropriateness), correct grammatical expression (agreement, tenses, sentence structure), clear and coherent development of ideas in a composition through an understanding of the process of writing from pre-writing through revision.

 Course Content

# Gujarati Studies [HS 115]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This course introduces non-Gujarati students to the basics of Gujarati Language (largely spoken, to a lesser extent, written), literature and culture. Starting with learning basic Gujarati language using the manual A step by step course towards mastering Gujarati, it will attempt to draw some insights into the psycho-Socio aspects of Gujarati linguistics. The course will also incorporate aspects of Gujarati culture and ethos as revealed in the literature, grammar, idioms, proverbs and poetry.

 The course will also introduce the students to the Basics Gujarati Literature and its thousand year old history. Through short tour of surrounding tourist spots of cultural, literary and linguistic significance, we also intend providing exposure to the Gujarati art and expression, beliefs and perceptions and if possible, taste and cuisine.

 Through basic exposure of Language, Literature and culture, the course intends taking one closer to the Gujarati world.

HS 191

#### World Civilizations and Cultures

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# World Civilizations and Cultures [HS 201]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Comprehensive overview of several important civilizational developments in the history of the world. The course will focus on how multiple cultures from around the globe have developed varying scientific, artistic and philosophical modes of knowledge in their pursuit to understand the human condition, society and the world at large. Civilizations to be covered in class include, but are not limited to, the Indus, Vedic, Mesopotamian, Egyptian, Han, Mayan, Aztec, Greek and Roman. Emphasis will be placed on the advancement and transmission of scientific knowledge, the implications for cross-cultural interactions, and the plurality of global thought.

 Course Content

# Global Political Economy [HS 417]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Economics and politics have always been inextricably interlinked. Kingdoms and nation states have survived and thrived on their ability to forge viable economies. Starting from Antoine de Montchrétien’s coining of the term, économie politique has defined world history in modern times. Developments such as industrialization, colonization, division of labor, development of infrastructure and evolution of institutions have transformed the world beyond recognition. In an era of globalization, political economy assumes more importance to understand the past, present and future.

HS 421

#### Classics of World Literature, Politics and Economics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Classics of World Literature, Politics and Economics [HS 422]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 In India, few understand the basis of the world we live in. Indian culture is deeply rooted not only in its ancient past but also influences from the Arab World, Iran, Europe and, now the US. Yet we rarely understand the fundamental ideas that underpin our society such as Montesquieu’s idea of separation of powers that emerged from the Enlightenment, the age of Voltaire. Similarly, few delve deep into what lies beneath the idea of the zero that is a product of the uniquely speculative genius of India. This will be a course that will introduce the great minds from literature and philosophy in the context of the society and politics of their time.

 This is a writing heavy class in which students will spend all their time reading original texts and penning essays on them. The topics that will be covered during the course are as follows:

 1. Historical context of the texts
 2. Cultural beliefs underpinning the classics
 3. Classics defining culture thereafter
 4. Philosophical assumptions of the various authors
 5. Economic circumstances in which these works were created
 6. Critical examination of the texts
 7. Great books as bedrock of collective imagination

HS 422

#### Writing Lab

#### Credit L | T | P | C 1 | 0 | 1.5 | 2

 Course Content

# Writing Lab [HS 423]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
1 | 0 | 1.5 | 2

 Critical thinking; intelligent reading; effective note taking; organizing thoughts; drawing up outlines writing practice; review and editing

 Critical Thinking
 Critical thinking means thinking for oneself and asking pertinent questions. Before penning anything, writers could begin by asking themselves three simple questions: Why am I writing? Who will read me? What impact do I want to have on my readers? Apart from questioning, critical thinking involves exploring multiple perspectives and engaging with divergent points of view. It involves understanding context, logic and argument. Only after deep, clear and hard thinking can writers pen something worthwhile. Therefore, the first session of the workshop is an initiation into critical thinking.

 Intelligent Reading and Effective Note Taking
 Reading involves not only deciphering the words on the page or screen, but also understanding what the writer is trying to say. This includes a feeling for the rhythm of the language that is inextricably interwoven with the writer’s ideas. Intelligent readers learn to pose a single question at every paragraph and page: What is the point that the writer is trying to make? Obviously, we comprehend not only speakers but also writers better when we make notes. There are a plethora of techniques to choose from, but jotting down relevant information concisely, precisely and clearly is the essence of good note taking. Therefore, the second session is a deep dive into techniques of reading and note taking.

 Organizing Thoughts, Drawing up Outline and Writing Clearly
 Following the note-taking exercises, participants choose issues, themes and topics to write on. This session involves deciding the subject matter of the writing exercise, developing ideas and then drawing up an outline. Participants will learn to develop clarity about the intent behind their writing, to know their audience, and to identify what they wish to communicate to relevant readers. This necessitates drawing up an outline, developing pertinent arguments and adducing key facts. In brief, participants in the third session will draw up a de facto communication strategy to attain clarity of purpose in their writing.

 Writing Exercise
 Once participants have validated their communication strategies with the facilitators, they will attack their writing exercise. This involves understanding the anatomy of writing and its components, including logic, drama, rhythm, rhetoric and structure. In the fourth session, participants will produce a first draft, fleshing out their skeletal outline.

 Proofreading, Citing Properly and Revision
 Nearly all first drafts are imperfect. They require reading, rereading, corrections and clarifications. Participants will learn that good writing includes proofreading and even rewriting. Critically evaluating one’s own work is both a science and an art. At its essence, it involves an appreciation of conveyed meaning. It also includes the ability to spot the divergence between intended and actual meaning. Along with proofreading, participants will focus on the importance of citing their sources. That is essential not only for getting the facts right, but also for developing credibility with readers. Publications judge writers on the quality of their sources and accuracy with which they cite them. This fifth and final session will demonstrate to participants that revising their initial drafts is like polishing diamonds. It is the final stage of a process that produces a gem.

 Writing Exercises followed by Reviews
 Finally, principles have to be applied and the proof of the pudding is in the eating. So, students will have one writing exercise after another in subsequent sessions followed by detailed reviews. Emails, cover letters, essays et al will feature one after another in these sessions.

HS 426

#### Introduction to Performance Studies

#### Credit L | T | P | C 2 | 0 | 2 | 4

 Course Content

# Introduction to Performance Studies [HS 427]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
2 | 0 | 2 | 4

 Theme #1: Representation vs. Performance: Distinguish between representation and performance by engaging with theories of Plato, Richard Schechner, Richard Bauman, Paulla Ebron, and Michael Taussig. Performance text: Dharamvir Bharati’s Andha Yug.

 Theme #2: Performativity: Learn about the theoretical paradigm of performativity which informs and organizes the discipline of performance studies. Theorists include JL Austin, Judith Butler, Uma Chakravarti, and James Baldwin. Performance text: Mahesh Dattani’s Dance like a Man.

 Theme #3: Ephemerality vs. Endurance: Investigate the ways in which performance is capable of revealing and restaging ephemeral histories by engaging with the debate between Peggy Phelan, Jose Munoz, Rebecca Schneider, Joseph Roach, and Diana Taylor. Also, Dwight Conquergood on performance ethnography. Performance text: Maya Rao’s Walk.

 Pedagogical strategy emphasizes active learning methods including inquiry-based, experiential, and embodied modes of engaging students in combination with orienting lectures. Specific instructional techniques include:

 1. Orienting lectures followed by two-way Q&A (clarifying questions from students for the instructor, and assessment questions from instructor to students)
 2. Staggered discussions in pairs, followed by small groups, and finally at class-level (combination of think-pair-share and jigsaw methods of discussion)
 3. Seminar discussion at class-level of performance texts and theoretical readings (preparatory readings pre-class followed by in-class discussion using an inquiry- based learning style)
 4. Workshop productions of performance texts (experiential and embodied mode of engagement in self-directed groups)

HS 428

#### Research Methods in Humanities and Social Sciences

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Research Methods in Humanities and Social Sciences [HS 505]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This is designed as a foundational course for those interested in in research in the Humanities and Social Sciences. It meant to familiarize students with a broad range of methods and analytical tools commonly employed in research in these fields.
 The course has been divided in to two broad components ; Quantitative and qualitative Methods. In Quantitative, method students will be taught how to conduct quantitative studies with focus on the understanding research questions, conceptual models, counter factual causal theory, confounding, mediation, moderation, measurement, scale development study designs and thread to validity of causal inferences.
 The quantitative segment the focus will be primarily on Ethnographic and interpretive methods and Historical research and Cultural Analysis. In the first pat ie, Ethnographic and interpretive research students will learn how to conduct interactive and Focus Group Discussions and analyze the data obtained from field surveys. In historical Research and cultural analysis, on the other hand students taught some basic conceptual and methodological issues pertaining to the historical discipline . the primary focus on the themes such as the ways and means of dealing with the object from the past. The manner of conducting archival research, various orders of evidence, the usefulness and method of studying orality and memory and the cultural significances of objects and images as well as the methods of using material for historical enquiry.

HS 505

#### Humanism, Anti-humanism, and Posthumanism

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Humanism, Anti-humanism, and Posthumanism [HS 507]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This course focuses on the emergence and legacies of Humanism into what is now called Posthumanism. We will discuss the origins of humanism, the various forms its takes, and its contemporary manifestations; various critiques of humanism (Anti-humanism); and new forms of humanism and ways in which it is being transformed, discarded or renewed (Posthumanism). The primary focus is on the question of what is the human, and what are its relations to others. To achieve this we approach a broad range of material concerning the human’s relation to animality, exceptionalism, knowledge, identity, subjectivity, technology, and gender, and how each of these relations shift as Humanism, Anti-humanism, and Posthumanism, draw and re-draw the boundaries between them.

 Keywords: Animality, Exceptionalism, Gender, Humanness and the Human, Knowledge, Ideology, Identity, Subjectivity, and Technology.

HS 507

#### Introduction to Indian Knowledge Systems

 Course Content

# Introduction to Indian Knowledge Systems [HS 508]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Indian knowledge systems, darshana, jñāna and vidyā, their continuous and cumulative nature; Traditions of argumentation (tarka), text interpretation, commentary and knowledge transmission; Social organisation of knowledge; Philosophical systems; Traditions of grammar and language; Systems of aesthetics, Nātyashāstra and the rasa theory; Systems of economy and polity (arthashāstra); Systems of ethics (nītishāstra) and values; Ayurveda; Systems of mathematics and astronomy, their nature, methods and accomplishments; Systems of chemistry and alchemy; Agriculture as an interplay of shāstra and loka paramparā; Systems of architecture (shilpashāstra) in principle and application; Systems of water harvesting and management; Systems of military science and martial arts.

HS 508

#### Social History of Dissent

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Social History of Dissent [HS 509]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This course will introduce students to conceptual issues concerning protest history or the history of dissent in India and other parts of the world in the eighteenth, nineteenth and twentieth centuries. The purpose of the course is to understand the constitutive factors determining different modes of protest. The course will pick and choose from a wide variety of movements such as the French Revolution, the Revolt of 1857, some of the nationalist movements that took place in Europe during this period, the nationalist movements in India led by the Indian National Congress and the numerous peasant and 'tribal' rebellions of the colonial period in India, The course will also discuss other modes of protest which have been equally and perhaps more effective at times than direct action in resisting subjection and bringing about change. Students will be exposed to conceptual issues such as the socio-political factors determining specific modes of protests, the role of the mob in a movement, the question of 'agency' or autonomy of marginalized groups in interpreting and changing their conditions and so on. The focus of the course may vary from year to year. However the emphasis will be mostly on protests by peasants, forest dwellers, factory workers, migrants and indigenous and colonized peoples.

HS 509

#### Perspectives on Indian Civilization

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Perspectives on Indian Civilization [HS 510]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Indus civilization: A description of the first civilization on the Indian subcontinent, its main features, achievements and legacy.
 Post-Harappan transition and the Aryan debate: Will explore the transition of the 2 nd millennium BCE, the grey area between India's two urbanizations.
 Gangetic civilization: Emerging in the early 1 st millennium BCE, it will lead to India's classical civilization; its treatment here will include several recent findings.
 Sacred geography and the making of India: The cultural unity of India was an important contribution of the Gangetic civilization, with the concept of sacred geography playing a major part. Early Indian society: A look at some of the dominant features of early Indian society, with inputs from literary, archaeological and epigraphic sources.
 Woman and child in ancient India: Will examine the place of woman and child in early Indian society as far as the sources allow us to reconstruct it.
 Knowledge systems and knowledge transmission: India early on attempted to organize and codify all knowledge in well-defined systems; this will explore the architecture of knowledge as well as the principal systems and their interaction.
 Main schools of thought and belief systems: Among the above knowledge systems, India's various philosophical and belief systems have pride of place and will be outlined here.
 Architecture and sacred geometry in classical India: This will explore the fundamental principles and practices of classical Indian architecture, as one example from the world of art.
 India's ecological traditions: A rarely highlighted but culturally important area, with increasing relevance to today's context.
 An overview of Science and Technology in ancient India: India's contributions to the world of science and technologies are either under-represented or misrepresented (positively or negatively); this will offer a fair portrayal of the high points in those fields.
 India's interface with other civilizations: Civilizations never live in isolation: India richly interacted with the rest of Asia and the Greek and Arab worlds, and later with Europe. This will explore those exchanges as well as the channels and methods of influence.
 Ethics and values: India developed its own systems of ethics and values; after studying their basic principles and some of their manifestations in Indian history, case studies will show how they survive to this day in diverse sections of Indian society.

HS 510

#### Political Thought

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Political Thought [HS 512]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 State and the social contract: Understanding the modern state and various theories about it; human nature and state of nature and the relationship between these, social contract theories; Justice: Modern conceptions of justice, basic tenets of John Rawls’s Theory of Justice, critiques and responses to Rawlsian theory by Amartya Sen, idea of fairness and justice, rejecting binary understanding of justice in favor of degrees; Liberalism and Communitarianism: Liberalism and its basic tenets, key ideals of communitarianism; communitarian critiques of liberalism, politics of Indian communitarianism, specifically Indian criticisms of liberalism in the communitarian tradition; Multiculturalism and Cosmopolitanism: Essential claims of multiculturalism in the West; key debates on citizenship in the context of immigrants, counterclaims of cosmopolitanism, toleration versus accommodation, contemporary cosmopolitanism debates; Civil Society: How is civil society theorized in the West and in India? What is its relation to democracy? ; Political Economy: Investigating the relationship between the state and market, theories of wealth creation and distribution, capitalism and its contradictions, the embedded nature of economics in society and culture; Critical theory approaches to democracy: Democracy and justice, Democracy and difference, deliberative democracy, and agonistic democracy. Secularism: meaning and origins of secularism in the West, Indian debates on secularism.

HS 518

#### Linguistic Anthropology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Linguistic Anthropology [HS 519]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Topic 1) Speech and language communities:

 --Difference between “speech” communities comprised of speakers with diverse linguistic repertoires, and ideological projection of ‘language’ community, or affiliation to one language

 Subtopic A) linguistic differentiation –how diverse speech practices crystallize into languages through ideological and semiotic social processes

 B) language socialization—how language becomes socialized through both linguistic and cultural activity

 C) language hegemonies—how certain varieties become to be seen as “prestigious” (language standard), their institutional circulation, and the resistance by certain communities to standardization processes

 Topic 2) Semiotics—examining language as a sign-producing activity integrated with the social, economic, and material world

 Subtopic A) political economy of language—how language is part of a political economic system, mediated through its sign-functions conceptions of value

 B) linguistic reflexivity—looking at how the metalinguistic function allows us to reflect on language use and make semiotic connections between language and social practice and ideologies

 C) scalar processes –the way language is semiotically scaled from micro to macro- and involved in large-scale processes like globalization

 D) voice and heteroglossia—the study of language as it indexes certain characters, stereotypes or “voices” [in a Bakhtinian sense) and the interplay of these voices in different genres

 Topic 3) Conversation analysis:

 The method used to describe face-to-face interaction

 A) multi-modal analysis –analysis of conversation beyond the utterance of words but also including gesture, gaze, etc.

 B) transcription methods—different ways of transcribing face-to-face interaction

 C) Face to face interaction—analysis of real time talk and assessing how this sheds light on social, cultural, cognitive aspects of language

 D) Performance—how to analyze performative uses of language and its implication for linguistic and cultural theory

 Topic 4) Language and thought

 The study of the way language may condition thought or vice-versa

 Subtopic A) Grammatical categories and cognition—Examination of the Sapir-Whorf Hypothesis and its criticisms; looking at experiments that relate how grammatical categories may influence perception of shape, colour, tacticity, etc.

 B) agency and intentionality—examining how agency and intentionality are grammatically embedded in language and what implication this may have on social construction

 Topic 5) Writing and literacy

 The study of language in its written mode, including attention to reading, writing, script, and materiality of language

 A) Visual language

 Examination of how the graphic aspects of language may offer different semiotic affordances than spoken language

 B) Textuality

 The way in which the idea of “text” is understood cross-culturally, and the intersection of oral and written speech in textual production

 C) Language and media

 The mediatization of language and its importance for changing literacy practice

 Topic 6) Language death and revitalization

 Investigation into the social conditions that cause languages to become endangered or die; whether languages should be revitalized or not; community politics around dead or dying langauges; and critical analyses of programmes of language revitalization.

HS 519

#### Ancient Indian Architecture

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 1 | 4

 Course Content

# Ancient Indian Architecture [HS 520]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 1 | 4

 Introduction: Tracing the beginning of architectural activities since prehistoric times, elements of Harappan Architecture and other contemporary Indian cultures.

 Concepts and Architectural Typologies: Textual references (e.g. Brhatsamhita and Samaranganasutradhara) and structural forms; broad characteristics of northern and southern styles (nagara, dravida and vesara); other styles like Bhumija, Kalinga and Varata; principles of composition; alignments and axes in temple forms

 Beginnings of Architectural forms: Evidences from the historical period onwards, early elements and forms; archaeological evidences; early architectural features like rock-cut, brick-built, wooden and other structural forms

 Rock-cut architectural forms: Tracing the beginnings of rock-cut architecture of Buddhist, Hindu and Jaina faiths, their evolution and elaboration during the early medieval period; critical analysis of significant examples of all faiths; the role of monasteries, royal patronages and ordinary worshippers in shaping the establishments; spread of architectural forms in various parts of India, influences and regional styles.

 Structural temples of the past: Architecture of the ancient temples of Buddhist, Hindu and Jaina faiths, their evolution and different styles; tracing their essential elements in the rock-cut architecture and transformation into structural forms; critical analysis of important architectural styles (nagara, dravida and vesara); the role of masons, sculptors and royal patronage; the influence of architectural styles in India and abroad, e.g. Kailasa of Ellora and Angkor Vat, Cambodia.

 Indo-Islamic Architectural forms: Introduction of a new style of architecture involving arches and domes in India; architecture of tombs, mosques; analysis of regional styles of Indo-Islamic architecture like Mughal, Jaunpur, Sharqi, Deccan, Qutb Shahi styles.

 Role of architectural heritage in establishing universal cultural values: Whose heritage? Need for preservation and dissemination; role of UNESCO in establishing norms for World Heritage Properties, symbols of universal cultural heritage.

 Field Trips and onsite Explanations: At the end of the course, field trips to important architectural centres in Gujarat, e.g. Sun Temple, Modhera and Rani-ki-Vav, Patan (both can be covered in a single day), will be arranged for the students to have onsite discussions and explanations. Apart from these architectural centres, a trip to Dholavira in the Kachchh district is also planned to expose the students to site management principles and practical approaches; discussions with the Site Managers at all these places will add further to their knowledge.

 The field trips will be mandatory and the students will submit individual detailed report of the field trip, which will be graded.

HS 520

#### Development Economics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Development Economics [HS 521]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

I. I. INTRODUCTION TO DEVELOPMENT ECONOMICS & SOME BASIC CONCEPTS
 • Nature of poverty across the world. Lives of the poor.
 • Meaning of poverty [GDP, utility, Sen’s Capabilities approach]
 • Meaning of Development, its indicators and determinants.
 II. II. FUNDAMENTALS OF THE EMPIRICAL METHODS OF DEVELOPMENT ECONOMICS – CAUSALITY VERSUS CORRELATION
 • Causation versus correlation. The idea of selection bias and the ‘Counterfactual’.
 • Introduction to the methods of Impact Evaluation:
 o Non-experimental (Before-After and Cross-sectional comparison)
 o Experimental (Randomized Control Trial - RCT)
 o Quasi-experimental (Instrumental Variable, Regression Discontinuity, Difference-in-Differences)
 III. TOPICS IN DEVELOPMENT ECONOMICS:
 1. Household Economics and intra-household decision-making: Considering households as independent economic units, focus will be on their decision-making processes given their resources – labour and capital.
 2. Population growth and Economic Development: After looking at population trends, the focus will be on its relations to economic development and on the policy prescriptions to control population.
 3. Labour and Unemployment: The definitions of employment, unemployment and labour force. Basic theories of formal and informal employment, and the labour markets in developing countries.
 4. Migration: Why do people migrate despite high urban unemployment [Harris-Todaro model]? What incentivizes migrants to send remittances? Role of policies in guiding migration.
 5. Education: Education as the first component of human capital. Role of human capital in economic development. Demand-side and supply-side determinants of schooling.
 6. Health and Nutrition: Health and nutrition as the second component of human capital. Demand-side and supply-side determinants of health achievements, and the related policies.
 7. Agricultural transformation and rural development: Role of agriculture vis-à-vis industry in economic development [The Lewis Model]. Microeconomics of farmer behaviour and agriculture.
 8. Sustainable development and Environment: Managing resource use while ensuring environmental sustainability. Role of negative externalities (market failures), property rights (private, open-access, CPR), environmental activities as public good. Kuznets Environmental curve, GDP and pollution.

III. IV. POLITICAL ECONOMY OF DEVELOPMENT
 9. Institutions, colonialism and path dependence: Meaning of institutions. New Institutional Economics and market failures. Examples of historical institutions (colonialism), and present institution (sharecropping).
 10. Policymaking, Governance, and the role of the state: Functionalist theory of the state – the social planner that maximizes social welfare. The pluralist theory of the state – special interest groups and electoral outcomes. Double cost of rent-seeking. Democracy and governance.

HS 610

#### South Asia: History, Culture and Society

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# South Asia: History, Culture and Society [HS 612]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This course provides a broad survey of fundamental developments in South Asian history, culture and society. The geographic focus of the class will be the Indian subcontinent and will include the peoples and histories of India, Pakistan, Bangladesh, Nepal and Sri Lanka. Critical ideas relating to social structures, religious practices, modes of polity and the production of art and literature will be explored from a historical perspective so as to highlight how these multi-dimensional cultural practices have both changed and remained constant over time and space. The course will examine events and ideas from ancient, medieval, early modern, colonial, post-colonial and contemporary South Asia. Themes of cultural adaptation, rejection, assimilation and transformation will provide a critical framework to understand how this culturally rich, complex and diverse region of the world has evolved over many millennia.

HS 624

#### Digital Cultures and New Media

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Digital Cultures and New Media [HS 631]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 The core focus of the course is to understand the significance of digital cultures and the role of new media in shaping public life and opinion. The discussion will revolve around creative dissent, strife, and the role of new media in the concerns for ‘peace’ and ‘justice’. The course will be conducted through a series of case-studies, field projects, and seminars.

 Some of the modules proposed to be covered in the course are as follows: (a) The story of the print media (case-studies of The Statesman, The Young India, The Harijan, The Telegraph); (b) The role of television (case-study of Doordarshan, case-study of exit polls and television ad-campaigns, the national debates, and the media trials); (c) Films of peace and strife (Issues of Censorship; case-studies of Judgment at Nuremberg, Rang De Basanti, Mr. and Mrs. Iyer, Firaaq); (d) Digital media: (Hyper-text and Blogging; the role of blogs and freedom of expression; photo-journalism and documentaries); (e) Social networks and micro- blogging (case-studies of Jan Lokpal movement, creating a tribe called IIT through social networks and blogs)

HS 632

#### Scarred Nations : Partition in the Indian subcontinent

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Scarred Nations : Partition in the Indian subcontinent [HS 633]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 The ramifications of the 1947 Partition in Bengal, Punjab and Sindh have been far-reaching. Recent scholarship interrogates the temporal marker of 1947 and suggests the persistence of fragmentation of memories and identities in postcolonial India. Topics covered include but are not limited to : Indian nationalism; moments of revivalism; economies of regions; from high-politics to testimonies; narrating the ‘other’; beyond the Hindu-Muslim binaries; divisions of the word, space, music, and histories; Partition in the East; displacement and memory in the West; the cinematic Partition; Partitions of contemporary India.

HS 635

#### Theories of social epidemiology

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Theories of social epidemiology [HS 636]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This course will introduce students to theoretical perspectives that explain the distribution and determinants of disease in society. The focus will be on the application of these theories to understand the impact of social determinants of health.
 History of public health; development of theories of disease distribution; historical and political influences on development of theoretical perspectives; ancient theories of health', traditional epidemiological models', individual-level health behaviour theories and models; social-ecological model; psychosocial theories; social production of disease (political economy of health); ecosocial theory; importance of theory; application of social epidemiological theories to disease distribution; implications for health policy.

HS 636

#### Disease, Health & Inequality

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Disease, Health & Inequality [HS 638]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 This course draws on medical anthropology, public health and development literature to examine the relationship between disease, health and inequality. The course will encourage students’ critical engagement in understanding the embodiment of inequality and the larger historical, social and economic forces that shape people’s health experiences globally. In particular, the course will begin with a discussion on the links between science and colonialism and subsequently move on to more contemporary debates on the inequalities of disease, suffering and infections (e.g. HIV/AIDS, Ebola and cancer), social determinants of health and illness, organ trafficking and commodification of human bodies, bioethics of global health practices and a critique of humanitarian aid and market-based medicine in the context of global health. The course will conclude with an examination of medicalized resistance to power and health as a human right.

Eastern India- Udaygiri, Khandagiri, Western Deccan- Bhaje, Pitalkhora, Kondivate, Kondane, Bedasa, Karle, Nasik, Kanheri, Junnar, Ajanta and Aurangabad, Eastern Deccan-Guntapalle. #### History of India, 1930-1964

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# History of India, 1930-1964 [HS 645]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C

 Classical Studies of Kinship and the Family; House societies; Substance exchange; Nature-culture divide; Queer kinship; Kinship and caste; Love and Intimacy; Kinship beyond the human

 Difference between commodity and gift; social bases of economic exchange; cross-cultural ideas of scarcity, leisure, transaction; kinship and economic systems

Module 4-Anthropology of the State
 State as a social and cultural construction, vertical vs. horizontal state structures; anthropology of bureaucracy, state policy as everyday practice

Module 4–Multisensorial Anthropology
 Anthropology and Phenomenology; socio-biological bases of sense perception; sense perception and social hierarchy; multimodal ethnography

Module 5–Anthropology of Time
 Anthropology and History; temporality as a social process; non-linear history; incorporating oral historiy into ethnographic research; historical events as ongoing cultural practice

Module 6–Posthuman Ethnography
 Multispecies anthropology; social ecology; social relations between humans and non-humans including animals, plants, and spirits; perspectivalism, communication between humans and non-human others.

Module 7–Decolonizing Anthropology
 Anthropology as a colonial enterprise, examples of collaborative anthropology and ethnography, indigenous anthropology; anthropological theory from the Global South

 L | T | P | C

Data selection and alignment: Types of evidence; Source analysis and appraisal; Evidence-based writing; Goal-driven breaking down and reformulation of message and argumentation; Meta-analytic approaches from primary sources.

IN 331

#### Human Evolution

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Human Evolution [IN 402]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 ‘We’, ‘Us’ or ‘Modern Humans’ are a consequence of our evolutionary past, traversing both biological and cultural interconnected developments. The course aims to inculcate in students an appreciation and understanding of how we became who we are today and recognize the current diversity in humankind through factual knowledge. This course will introduce students to some of the fundamental theories, debates, concepts, methods and nomenclatures that contribute to the study of human evolution. Students will be able to reconstruct our bio-cultural history through multidisciplinary and scientific perspectives including evolutionary biology, palaeoanthropology, prehistoric archaeology, and genetics.

 Topic 1: Brief introduction to subjects, theories, methods and concepts which are employed in the study of human evolution.

 • Scope of subjects such as physical anthropology, palaeoanthropology and prehistoric archaeology.
 • History of evolutionary thought – Cuvier, Hutton, Lyell, Linnaeus, Lamarck, Darwin, Mendel, Huxley. Concepts of adaptation, natural selection, speciation, mutation, heredity and variation. Micro and macro evolution.
 • Linnaean system of classification.
 • Geological time scale, prehistoric timeline, importance of context and material culture in Archaeology, relative and absolute dating techniques and methods.
 • Miocene, Pliocene and Pleistocene epoch environmental settings.
 • Human osteology and morphometrics, process of fossilization and taphonomy.

 Topic 2: Assessment of the human fossil record: tracing the major stages of human evolution during the Miocene- Pliocene – Early Pleistocene epochs (between 23 and 5 million years ago to 1.2 million years ago).

 • Primate evolution.
 • Hominoidea superfamily; divergences between- Hominidae and Hylobatidae families, Homininae and Ponginae sub-family, Hominini and Gorillini tribe, Hominina and Panina sub-tribe.
 • The earliest hominins- Sahelanthropus, Orrorin and Ardipithecus.
 • Genus Australopithecus, Kenyanthropus and Paranthropus.
 • Key fossil finds (discovery, prehistoric sites, context and dating); their origins; description and comparative assessment of their biological, behavioural and adaptive characteristics.

 Topic 3: Assessment of the human fossil record: tracing the major stages of evolution of our own genus during the Pleistocene (2.5 million years ago to 12,000 years before present) epoch.

 • The earliest Homo and the earliest archaeological record
 • From Homo ergaster to Modern Homo sapiens.
 • Recent Homo species discoveries from South Africa and South East Asia
 • Key fossil finds (discovery, prehistoric sites, context and dating); their origins; description and comparative assessment of their biological, behavioural, adaptive and cultural characteristics.

 Topic 4: Special lectures on

 • Out of Africa theory and the extant human diversity (one lecture by Sharmistha Majumdar)
 • Using DNA to trace the most recent common ancestor (one lecture by Umashankar Singh)

 The course will be taught through lectures based on published and online sources along with documentary viewing followed by discussions, human osteology laboratory sessions, museum/field visits and workshops for experimental replication of stone, bone, shell and ceramic objects.

All about Matrices II

Inner Products

Engineering method and statistical thinking
 Engineering method and statistical thinking; collecting engineering data; Mechanistic and Empirical models; Probability and Probability models
Probability
 Sample space and events; axioms of probability; additional rules; conditional probability; multiplication and total probability rules; Bayes theorem
Discrete and Continuous Random variables and their distributions
Random variables, Distribution Functions, Discrete random variables, probability mass functions (p.m.f.), Continuous random variables, probability density functions (p.d.f.), Expectations, mean, variance, moments, examples
Point estimation of parameters and sampling distributions
 Method of moments, maximum likelihood estimators
Statistical Intervals
 Confidence intervals on mean and variance; Guidelines for constructing confidence intervals.
Hypothesis testing
 Tests on Mean, variance and standard deviation of normal distributions, Inference
 procedures for a single sample
Linear Regression:
 Empirical Models, Simple Linear Regression, Least Square Estimators, Confidence intervals in regression, prediction of new observations
Design and analysis of single-factor experiments:
 Analysis of variance

III-A. Syllabus
Introduction to engineering optimization
 Introduction to optimisation problems in engineering, convex sets, convex functions, and their properties
Multivariate optimization
 No constraints, equality constraints, and inequality constraints; Kuhn Tucker Conditions
Linear programming
 Simplex method, basic feasible solution, optimal solution, duality and complementary slackness, primal-dual simplex algorithm, Linear integer programming problem

MA 629

#### Measure Theory & Probability

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1.5 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Measure Theory & Probability [MA 630]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1.5 | 0 | 4

#### Basic theory of measure and integration:

- Sigma algebras, measures, basic properties of measures, construction of measures (sketch), examples (Lebesgue measure on the real line).
- Measurable functions and integration. Simple functions, properties of measurable functions, (Lebesgue) integration, limit theorems for integration (Fatou’s Lemma, Monotone Convergence Theorem, Dominated Convergence Theorem), comparison with Riemann integrals.
- Absolute continuity, Radon-Nikodym theorem (statement), functions of bounded variation.
- Convergence of sequences of measurable functions.
- Product measures and the Fubini-Tonelli theorems (statements only).

#### Basic theory of probability:

- Motivating examples from the discrete setting.
- Probability spaces, events, probability measures, basic properties.
- Random variables. Distributions functions and properties, densities and mass functions, examples.
- Expectation. Mean, variance, moments, moment generating functions and properties.
- Independence and conditioning. Borel-Cantelli Lemmas, conditional expectations and probabilities given sigma algebras, properties.
- Convergence of random variables. Weak law of large numbers, Strong law of large numbers (statement only), Central limit theorem (statement only).

MA 632

#### Theory of Partitions

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1.5 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Theory of Partitions [MA 633]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1.5 | 0 | 4

 Fundamental theorems in q-series and theta functions – q-binomial theorem, Jacobi triple product identity, Euler’s pentagonal number theorem, Ramanujan’s 1Ψ1 summation formula, Quintuple product identity and its special cases.

 Applications of the elementary series-product identities to partitions - combinatorial techniques in partition theory – through generating functions, and through bijective proofs, Gaussian polynomials and their properties.

 Congruences for p(n) and the associated partition Statistics - congruences for the partition function p(n) modulo 5, 7, and 11, ranks, cranks – their properties and their generalizations,

 Restricted partition functions - connections with q-series, modular forms and mock modular forms

 Rogers-Ramanujan identities - analytic and combinatorial versions, applications in various areas.

 The Hardy-Ramanujan-Rademacher formula for p(n) – proof and applications

Miscellaneous topics in partitions – Macmahon’s partition analysis, plane partitions, recent advances in partition theory.

MA 634

#### Perturbation Methods

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Perturbation Methods [MA 635]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Asymptotic Approximations: Asymptotic expansions; Regular perturbation problems (including the domain perturbation problems, e.g., and singular perturbation problems; Asymptotic solution of algebraic, transcendental, and differential equations.
 Matched Asymptotic Expansion: Outer and inner solutions, matching, composite expansion; Boundary layers; Interior layers; Corner layers; Partial differential equations – elliptic problem, parabolic boundary layer.
 Multiple Scales: Two and three-time scales; Forced motion near resonance; Weakly coupled oscillators; Slowly varying coefficients; Boundary layers; Linear and nonlinear waves and their applications, application to porous media flow.
 The Wentzel-Kramers-Brillouin (WKB) theory: Turning points – multiple turning points; Wave propagation and energy methods; Wave propagation and slender-body approximations; Ray methods – WKB expansion, surfaces, and wavefronts, Eikonal equation, transport equation, ray equation; Discrete WKB method, application to potential barrier problems.
 Applications: There will be significant emphasis on highlighting applications of these topics in fluid mechanics, electrodynamics, quantum mechanics. Some of these examples can also be discussed at the discretion of the instructor, if time permits.

 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Definition of a matrix Lie group and its examples; Topological properties; connectedness, compactness, and simply connectedness of certain matrix Lie groups; Lie algebra of a matrix Lie group, exponential mapping, complexification of a Lie algebra; The Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula, the connection between Lie group homomorphism and Lie algebra homomorphism; Basic representation theory, direct sum and tensor product of representations, Dual representation, Complete reducibility, Hopf algebra of matrix elements of finite dimensional representations; The representation of the Lie algebra su(2); Semisimple Lie algebra, Roots and Root spaces, Weyl Group, Root system; Highest weight Representation of a semisimple Lie algebra, Construction of representations through Verma module; Classification of simple Lie algebras, a glimpse into Kac-Moody algebras. Applications of matrix Lie groups, for example, SU(2), SU(3), SO(3), SO(3,1), to physics.

MA 638

#### Matrix Analysis

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Matrix Analysis [MA 639]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Review: Spectral theorem, singular value decomposition, Moore-Penrose inverse, least squares solutions.
 Variational principles of spectrum: The Minmax principle for spectrum of Hermitian matrices, Rayleigh–Ritz theorem, Courant–Fischer theorem, Weyl’s monotonicity theorem, Cauchy interlacing theorem, inertia and congruence, Sylvester’s law of inertia.
 Matrix norms: Definition and examples of matrix norms, Gelfand formula, location of eigenvalues, Greschogorin theorem. Applications to sensitivity of linear systems.
 Positive semidefinite and Positive definite matrices: Properties and characterizations, the Loewner partial order relation, operator monotone and operator convex functions, Hadamard’s inequality, Fischer’s inequality. Applications to dynamical systems.
 Perron–Frobenius theory: Matrices with positive and non-negative entries. Applications to Page rank algorithm and steady state of a stationary finite Markov chain.

MA 639

#### Integral Geometry in Imaging Sciences

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Integral Geometry in Imaging Sciences [MA 640]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction to integral transforms: A basic model for tomography, Tomography, Beer’s
 law, and X-ray tomography.
  The Radon transform: The Radon transform, Inversion of the Radon transform, the
 Central slice theorem, the Radon Inversion Formula, Backprojection, Filtered
 Backprojection, an alternate formula for the Radon inverse, the Hilbert transform, mapping
 properties of the Hilbert transform, Approximate inverses for the Radon transform, the
 range of the Radon transform, data with bounded support, Continuity of the Radon
 transform and it’s inverse, estimates for the inverse transform, the higher dimensional
 Radon transform.
  X-ray transform: Definition and elementary properties of X-ray transform, Inversion
 formulas, the interplay between X-ray transform, Fourier transform, and convolution,
 Uniqueness, the ranges, stability estimates.
  The Doppler transform and its generalizations: Definition and some properties of
 Doppler transform, kernel description and injectivity modulo the natural kernel, invertibility,
 and discussion of the range of the Doppler transform.
  Incomplete data and support theorem: Support theorems for the Radon and X-ray
 transform. The limited angle problem, the exterior problem, the interior problem, and the
 restricted source problem.
  Current state of the art: A brief survey of generalization of these transforms to tensor
 fields and some ongoing research problems.

 L | T | P | C

ME 206

#### Fluid Dynamics

 Course Content

# Fluid Dynamics [ME 207]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1.5 | 1.5 | 5

 Introduction, history, and applications of fluid dynamics: Properties: continuum approximation, Knudsen number, density, specific gravity, pressure, vapor pressure, compressibility, speed of sound, viscosity, surface tension; Fluid kinematics: Lagrangian vs Eulerian descriptions, flow patterns and visualization, linear and volumetric strains, shear strain, and vorticity; Reynolds transport theorem, integral and differential forms of conservation of mass, momentum (linear and angular), and energy; Navier-Stokes equations and exact solutions; Bernoulli’s equations, applications and its relation to conservation of energy; Dimensional analysis and modeling: Dimensional analysis and similarity, method of repeating variables and the Buckingham Pi theorem, experimental testing and modeling; Reynolds number and qualitative description of different flow regimes: laminar vs turbulent flow, creeping flow, inviscid flow, irrotational flow, and boundary layers; Internal flow: Boundary layer development, laminar and turbulent flow in pipes, major and minor losses, piping networks and pump rating; External flows: concept of drag and lift, flow separation, friction and pressure drag, drag coefficients; Introduction to compressible flows: Definition of compressible flow, Mach number and flow regimes, Quasi-1D isentropic flows, area-velocity relation, flow in nozzles and diffusers, choking, qualitative description of shock and expansion waves; Solution of flow problems using CFD softwares. Laboratory: The laboratory component will complement the theory component of the course. The students will learn to do the following measurements and visualizations: pressure measurement; velocity and flow rate measurement; flow visualization The acquired skills and techniques will in turn be used to critically examine and understand the concepts covered in the theory component of the course

Hairpin Vortices.

 Turbulent Flows. Classical experiments/observations, Kolgomorov’s scale. closure problem, correlations and spectra. RANS, Reynolds stress. Wall bounded and free shear flows. vorticity in turbulent flows, jet flows. kinetic energy budget, turbulent kinetic energy production and cascading, Eddy viscosity, mixing length, turbulent dispersion. Some insight into current activities for turbulence modeling.

ME 628

#### Compressible Flow

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Compressible Flow [ME 635]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Review of the governing equations of compressible flow and Thermodynamic concepts. Forms of energy equation for compressible flow. Wave propagation speed in ideal gases. Isentropic flow. Stagnation Flow Properties. Critical Flow Properties. Compressible frictionless flow in a shock tube and variable area ducts based on one-dimensional Euler Equations. Normal Shocks and Rankine-Hugoniot conditions. Oblique shock waves. Expansion Waves. Prandtl-Meyer Function. Shock-Expansion Theory for external compressible flow past bodies. Conical Flows. Internal compressible flow in constant area and variable area ducts with heat transfer and with friction leading to notions of Rayleigh and Fanno Flows. Combustion Waves. Condensation Shocks. Compressible Potential Flow Theory. Vorticity considerations. Notions of compressible velocity potential. Development of low-order compressible flow models for high speed flows based on linearized small-disturbance potential flow theory for subsonic, transonic and supersonic flows. Similarity Rules. Brief insight into hypersonic flow and rarified gas dynamics concepts. Disturbance behavior in unsteady compressible flow. Notions of Compressible Boundary Layers. Crocco’s Theorem. Shock wave boundary layer interaction. High temperature flows. Aerodynamic Heating.

 Course Content

# Principles of Business Management [MS 306]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 What exactly is “business management”? What does a manager do? Can management principles be applied to non-business situations? Is management an “art’ or a ‘science”? This introductory course aims to address some of these questions. It provides a broad overview of management and its functions. The topics covered are as below:

 Evolution of Business Management as a discipline; Introduction to basic concepts in Management; Primary functions of management: Planning; Organising/Staffing; Directing/leading; Controlling; Management in context: Small v/s large organizations, management in different cultural contexts; introduction to some “popular” management tools/concepts – strategy, core competence, re-engineering, centralization/decentralization.

MS 404

#### Business Skills for Entrepreneurs

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Business Skills for Entrepreneurs [MS 406]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Field: This is an advanced entrepreneurship course for those who have already completed MS403. It is intended for students to develop an in-depth understanding of how to manage a start-up.

 It will be taught as a case study based instruction method. After students are exposed to the cases, the successes and failures will be analyzed in class in the context of the skills being discussed and students would be required to answer questions as class-work or homework. The cases may be presentations by entrepreneurs or written cases.

 In this first half of the semester, case studies will primarily focus on corporate culture, customer development, team building and negotiations, four essential elements of entrepreneurship. The second half of the semester will primary focus on idea generation, product management and financial planning & control.

 The course also allows students to start a new project or continue to develop their concept started in MS403. In addition to the instructors, visit by successful entrepreneurs, guest lectures and visits to start-ups will be organized to enhance learning.

 The course focuses on meeting customer needs and requirements of quality, on-time delivery, cost, and service/flexibility.

MS 407

#### Financial Considerations in Engineering Decisions

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 Course Content

# Financial Considerations in Engineering Decisions [MS 408]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 2

 This course is designed to train students to develop an in-depth perception of major considerations in decision making involving large engineering projects. Students will be presented with tools of finance and decision making and interactively lead through practical applications. The course concludes with a case study involving a real world example. A primer will be provided on the technology relevant to the keystone case.

 Interpretation of Financial Statements: Concise review of corporate financial statements with practical applications from start-ups to mature corporations. Special emphasis on the interpretation of financial statements and the application of such interpretations to corporate decision making with emphasis on technology projects.

 Applied Decision Making: Introduction to decision making models with interactive exercises on canonical applications.

 Case study methodology: methodology review and in-class interactive case study by example.

 Review of Relevant Technology: The keystone case involves in its core, a technology milestone. A primer with examples and illustrations of that technology will be presented so that the students may perform an informed in-depth case analysis.

 The Case: Case study involving a major real life technology breakthrough and various decisions that lead to the accomplishment of the goal will be presented through a case summary. Students will be assigned different topics of analysis and would be guided through their independent analyses of the corporate decision making process taking into detailed consideration, the financial as well as technology aspects. As a bonus, the students will be given the opportunity to make predictions on alternate outcomes and their potential impact on the companies as well as society.

MSE 203

#### Transport Phenomena in Materials Engineering

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Transport Phenomena in Materials Engineering [MSE 204]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Introduction to transport phenomena
 Concepts of momentum transport: Newton’s law of viscosity and non-Newtonian fluids. Mathematical framework for Continuity equation, Navier-Stokes equations, Hagen-Poiseullie’s equation, Stokes law etc. Flows: laminar flow, turbulence, flow in porous beds.
 Applications of momentum transport in ceramic suspensions and polymer solutions, oil refineries, flow in porous membranes/electrodes.
 Concepts of heat transport: Fundamentals of conduction heat transfer, convection heat transfer, radiative heat transfer. Fourier’s law, steady and unsteady state conduction laws and governing equations. Heat transfer coefficient in convection. Radiation, Fick’s law of diffusion, mass transfer coefficient
 Applications of heat transport in materials processing namely heat treatment, solidification, casting, cooling of slabs, crystal growth, heat losses from the furnace, alloy homogenization, segregation, oxidation, reduction, microfluidics, cooling in electronic devices, flow and vacuum production.
 Concepts of mass transport: Fundamentals of diffusion; Fick’s first law and second law for steady and unsteady diffusion, uphill diffusion and Kirkendal's effect.
 Fundamentals of convective mass transfer; free and forced convective mass transfer, Convective mass transfer, rate laws and mass transfer coefficient.
 Application of mass transfer in case hardening of metals, doping of semi-conductors, homogenization, oxidation, absorption/desorption of gases in liquid metals.

MSE 205

#### Physics of Materials

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Physics of Materials [MSE 206]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Introduction, review of crystal lattices and bonding, Reciprocal lattice, Wigner Seitz cells, Brillouin zones. Metals: Electronic and Thermal Properties: The Drude (free electron) model, The relaxation-time approximation, Ohm’s law, Classical derivation of resistivity, Different methods of measuring resistivity, Classification of crystalline solids based on electrical measurement, Failure of Drude model, Electronic heat capacity, Thermal conductivity, and The Weidemann-Franz law, Hall effect; Sommerfeld model: The introduction of quantum mechanics, Fermi energy, The Fermi-Dirac function, Electronic density of states, The electronic heat capacity, Success and failure of the Sommerfeld model. Concept of phonons and specific heat, Einstein and Debye Models, Debye temperature, Applications. Schrödinger equation for a free electron (with no potential), Equation for bound electrons in a solid and its relevance to periodic potential, Nearly free electron model, Energy bands, Effective mass, Distinction between metals, semiconductors, and insulators, Applications. Optical properties, direct and indirect bandgap semiconductors, Applications. Superconductivity, Type I, and Type II, Meissner effect, Critical current and Critical magnetic field, Applications.

 L | T | P | C

 Course Content

# Introduction to Quantum Materials and Devices [MSE 318]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 3

MSE 354

#### Surface Engineering

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Surface Engineering [MSE 355]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 1. Fundamentals on surface energy, surface tension, polar and apolar materials, viscosity, vapor pressure, enthalpy of vaporization

 2. Surface properties: roughness, flatness, topography, chemical composition/surface chemistry, bond structure

 3. Introduction to wetting, tribology, capillarity and biofouling

 4. Types of fluids: pure liquids, emulsions, suspensions, Newtonian & non-Newtonian, biological and classification of real-life fluids such as blood, paints, ketchup, oil, yogurt etc.

 5. Theories: Young’s equation, Wenzel theory, Cassie-Baxter theory, Zisman plot, van Oss-Chaudary- Good theory, Gibbs free energy minimization, van der Waals forces namely Debye, Keesom, London forces as well as hydrogen bonding

 6. Surface modifications by chemical ways: dip coating, spraying, sputtering, silanization treatments, carburizing, nitriding, physical vaporization deposition, chemical vaporization method etc.

 7. Engineering surface topography: micromachining, e- beam lithography, photolithography, laser ablation, chemical etching, curing, replication, hot embossing etc.

 8. Surface characterization techniques: SEM, CA goniometer, AFM, optical profilometer, frictional measurements-oscillating tribometer, sphere on disc and pin on disc, Nanoindentation

 9. Bioinspired strategies for tuning surface adhesion: Lotus leaf effect, butterfly surfaces, rose petals, Nepenthes Pitcher plant, gecko-lizard, underwater oleophobicity by fish scales

 10. Tribology: Stribeck curve, lubrication, influence of viscosity, load, sliding speed on dry and lubricated friction and wear of materials. Archard’s wear principle and wear mechanisms

 11. Applications and emerging challenges

PH 202

#### Solid State Physics

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Solid State Physics [PH 203]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

Module 1: Introduction to Quantum Mechanics and Thermal Physics. (with the perspective to introduce electronic and thermal properties of materials) Wavefunction, Schrodinger’s equation and its solution to simple potentials; Equipartition theorem, Boltzmann distribution; Quantum and classical distribution functions.

Module 2: Electronic properties of materials. Classical models and their limitations; Origin of bands, Fermi energy, Fermi surface, density of states, conductivity; Metals and insulators; Magnetic moment, Exchange energy and Hund’s rules; Origin of magnetism, Para- Dia- and Ferro-magnetism.

Module 3: Thermal properties of materials. Vibration of one-dimensional monoatomic and diatomic chain, Group velocity, Normal modes, Phonons (optical and acoustic); Phonon heat capacity, Planck distribution, Debye T3 law, Einstein model; Thermal conductivity of phonon gas.

Module 4: Physics of Semiconductors. Energy bands in semiconductors, band gap; Electrons, Holes, Effective mass; Silicon and Germanium; Conductivity and Mobility of carriers.

Module 5: Introduction to Superconductivity. Electrodynamics of superconductor, London equations, Meissner effect; Type I and type II superconductors; Cooper pairs, energy gap, coherence length; Josephson effect.

PH 203

#### Quantum Technologies – Applications

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Quantum Technologies – Applications [PH 204]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1 | 0 | 4

 Overview and Motivation – Introduction to the landscape of quantum technologies, including quantum computing, sensing, communication, and quantum materials; historical development and motivation, with emphasis on identifying the core physical principles that enable quantum technological advantage and how material platforms underpin later developments; Quantum States, Control, and Open Systems – Physical realization of qubits as two-level systems; control and manipulation; density matrix formalism; decoherence, relaxation, and dephasing (T₁ and T₂ processes); introduction to open quantum systems and noise channels, with illustrations from superconducting qubits, trapped ions, and other platforms; Measurement and Quantum Resources – Quantum measurement theory including projective measurement, backaction, and basic ideas of weak measurement; quantum resources such as coherence, superposition, entanglement, and squeezing, and their operational roles across quantum technologies; Quantum Limits and Metrology – Fundamental limits to measurement precision including the standard quantum limit and Heisenberg scaling; role of quantum correlations and squeezing in enhancing sensitivity; applications to atomic clocks, interferometry, and gravimetry; Quantum Information and Noise – Classical and quantum information concepts including Shannon entropy and von Neumann entropy; qualitative understanding of quantum channels, noise, and information flow; implications for communication and computation; Quantum Computation – Principles and Realizations – Quantum gates and circuits; many-body Hilbert space and the origin of computational advantage; overview of algorithms such as Shor’s algorithm; realistic considerations including NISQ devices, noise, and error correction; critical assessment of quantum advantage; Quantum Communication – Information and Implementations – Principles of quantum communication including no-cloning, security, and eavesdropping; basic ideas of quantum key distribution; physical implementations in fibre, free-space, and satellite platforms; Platforms, Materials, and Scalability – Comparative overview of leading hardware platforms including superconducting systems, semiconductor and spin-based systems, atomic and photonic platforms, and defect-based systems; emphasis on how quantum materials and device physics enable control while limiting scalability; Limits, Challenges, and Outlook – Fundamental and practical limitations of quantum technologies including decoherence, scalability, and control; realistic assessment of near-term and long-term prospects across computing, sensing, and communication.

#### Quantum Sensing

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 1.5 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Quantum Sensing [PH 301]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 1.5 | 0 | 4

 Classical sensing and measurement—physical signals, sensors, data acquisition, and noise sources (thermal, shot, and 1/f), along with signal processing techniques including filtering and lock-in detection, parameter estimation, Fisher information and the Cramér–Rao bound; accompanied by laboratory sessions using Arduino/Raspberry Pi platforms for sensor interfacing, noise characterization, signal detection, and estimation; Quantum theory recap—quantum postulates, projective measurements, positive operator-valued measures (POVMs), quantum noise, measurement backaction, weak and continuous measurements, and quantum non-demolition measurements; Limits to precision — standard quantum limit, its origin in the trade-off between measurement imprecision and backaction, and illustrative examples from interferometry (Ramsey protocol) and position measurements (Optomechanics); Quantum estimation theory—quantum Fisher information, quantum Cramér–Rao bound, and optimal measurement strategies; Quantum resources for sensing— coherent, Fock, and squeezed states, entanglement-enhanced sensing and Heisenberg scaling; Platforms for quantum sensing—atomic systems, interferometric techniques, ultracold atoms, cavity QED systems, solid-state platforms such as NV centers, and quantum detection methods.

# Introduction to the Special Theory of Relativity [PH 406]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

#### Waves and Oscillations

#### Credit L | T | P | C 3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 Course Content

# Waves and Oscillations [PH 408]

 Credit
 L | T | P | C
3 | 0 | 0 | 4

 ● Periodic Motions: Simple harmonic oscillator (SHO), Superposition of vibrations: Lissajous Figures, Parallel and perpendicular superposition, examples of free vibrations in physical system: mass-spring system, Young Modulus, Pendulum etc.

 ● Forced Vibrations: Undammed oscillator with force term, Forced oscillation with damping, Transient phenomena, Examples of resonance: Electrical, optical, nuclear resonance, Anharmonic oscillator.

 ● Coupled Oscillators: Introduction of the concept of normal modes, natural frequency, forced vibrations and resonance of two coupled oscillator, generalization of N coupled oscillator: finding normal modes for N coupled oscillator, Longitudinal oscillations, normal modes for crystal lattice, stretched string, Longitudinal vibrations of a rod, vibrations of air columns, elasticity of gas, normal modes of two and three dimensional systems, Fourier analysis of normal modes problem, quasi normal modes and dissipation.

 ● Progressive Waves: Travelling waves, progressive waves in specific media, superposition of wave pulses, Dispersion; phase and group velocities, transport of energy by the wave, momentum flow and mechanical radiation pressure, Polarization, solutions of the wave equation in even and odd dimensions.

 ● Interference: The Huygens-Fresnel Principle, Reflection and refraction of plane waves, Doppler effect, Double slit interference, Diffraction by single slit, Diffraction grating, Fraunhofer vs Fresnel diffraction.
